Remark 7.12

(On non-uniform compact support)
.

An important aspect of Definition
7.7
is that the compact support
condition in
(7.7a)
is not uniform over all

{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
. This creates some difficulties in the
computation of
\mathbf{D}_{G}(\mathbf{M})
for
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
in
Proposition
8.5
(which would be easier using a uniform
compact support condition). But the non-uniformness will be crucial for the
construction of the required transfer in Theorem
6.7
41
41

41

This
issue comes also up in proofs of the Farrell–Jones Conjecture for certain discrete
groups, but somewhat less visible. For example the category
\mathcal{D}^{G}(Y;\mathcal{A})

in
[
9
, Sec. 3.3]
does use a uniform compact support
condition, but the proof later also uses the category

\mathcal{O}^{G}(Y,(Z_{n},d_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
where the compact support condition is not uniform
in
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
. The construction of the transfer depends on certain maps

X\to|\mathbf{J}_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}}|^{\wedge}
where
X
is the extended Bruhat-Tits building associated to
G
,
see Theorem
D.1
. The construction of these maps uses an intermediate step
maps
X\to\mathit{FS}(X)
, where
\mathit{FS}(X)
is the flow space associated to
X
, see
Subsection
D.iii
. In the construction of these latter maps the
geodesic flow on
\mathit{FS}(X)
is used for arbitrary long times. Roughly, this has the
effect that the images of
X
in
\mathit{FS}(X)
are spread out over large parts of
\mathit{FS}(X)

and ultimately we do not have uniform control over the images of the maps

X\to|\mathbf{J}_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}}|^{\wedge}
. This forces us to work with the non-uniform compact
support condition over
|\Sigma|
. This is also the reason for the non-uniform nature
of
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(P)
in Definition
5.8
. The non-uniformness of this compact
support condition in turn force us to work with
P
-foliated control over
|P|^{\wedge}

instead of continuous control of
|P|
, compare Remark
5.4
.

Remark 7.13
.

In contrast to the compact support conditions, the finite dimensional support condition
is uniform in
\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
. This is crucial for (and directly implies) the skeleton
continuity of
\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}
in Proposition
8.22
.

Definition 7.14
.

For
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}
we define
\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})
as the collection of all
subsets
Y
of
|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
satisfying the following
condition: there is
k_{0}
such that for each
t_{0}\geq k_{0}
there is
k_{1}
with

Y\cap|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times\{t_{0}\}\times\mathbb{N}_{\geq k_{1}}=\emptyset.

Definition 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a category with
G
-support. For
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}
we apply
Definition
4.20
and define

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma}),\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma}));

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}),\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})).

Often we drop
\mathcal{B}
from the notation and write

\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})=\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})
and

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})=\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})
.

Remark 7.16
.

The category
\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
can be described slightly more explicit as
follows. Objects of
\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
are objects of

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma}))
. Morphisms in
\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
are equivalence
classes of morphisms in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma}))
, where

\varphi,\psi\colon(S,\pi,B)\to(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},B^{\prime})
are identified, if and on if there is

Y\in\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})
such that

\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}=\psi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}

whenever
s\in S
,
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
satisfy
\pi(s),\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime})\not\in Y
.

Remark 7.17

(
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
as sequences)
.

An advantage of
\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})
over
\mathbf{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
is that it
admits the following description.
For
{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
we can restrict to

|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times\{{\underline{t}}\}
and obtain a functor

\res_{\underline{t}}\colon\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}))\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}).

Write
{\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\textstyle\prod}}_{\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})

for the following category. Objects are sequences

(\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
of objects in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})
. Morphisms are equivalence classes of
sequences
(\varphi_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
of
morphisms in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})
, where two sequences

(\varphi_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
,

(\varphi^{\prime}_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
are equivalent
if there is
k_{0}
such that for any
t_{0}\geq k_{0}
there is
k_{1}
such that for all

t_{1}\geq k_{1}
we have
\varphi_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}
. The above restrictions
combine to a faithful functor

(7.18)

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\;\to\;\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}).

A sequence
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
of
objects in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})
is in the image
of (
7.18
), if and only if the following four conditions are
satisfied:

(7.18a)

\supp_{1}\mathbf{B}=\big\{(z,{\underline{t}})\;\big|\;z\in\supp_{1}\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}}\big\}\in\mathfrak{D}^{0}_{1}({\bf\Sigma})
;

(7.18b)

\supp_{2}\mathbf{B}=\Big\{{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime},{\underline{t}}}\\
{z,{\underline{t}}}\end{pmatrix}}\;\Big|\;{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime}}\\
{z}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}}\Big\}\in\mathfrak{D}^{0}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
;

(7.18c)

\supp_{G}\mathbf{B}=\bigcup_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\supp_{G}\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}}\in\mathfrak{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
;

(7.18d)

\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}}
is finite for
all
{\underline{t}}
42
42

42

This condition comes from the finite over points
condition in
(4.12a)
and the finiteness over
\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}

in
(7.7a)
.
.

A sequence of morphisms is in the image of (
7.18
) if and only
if it is equivalent to a sequence

(\varphi_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
of morphisms in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})
satisfying

(7.18e)

\supp_{2}\varphi=\big\{{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime},{\underline{t}}}\\
{z,{\underline{t}}}\end{pmatrix}}\;\big|\;{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime}}\\
{z}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\varphi_{\underline{t}}\big\}\in\mathfrak{D}^{0}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
;

(7.18f)

\supp_{G}\varphi=\bigcup_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\supp_{G}\varphi_{\underline{t}}\in\mathfrak{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
.

For
{\bf\Sigma}=(\Sigma,P)\in\mathcal{R}
and
Q\in\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
we have

as a special case
of (

.

Lemma 7.19
.

{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}

Definition 7.20
.

For a category
\mathcal{B}
with
G
-support we define the two functors

\mathbf{D}_{G}(\--;\mathcal{B}),\,\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}(\--;\mathcal{B})\;\colon\mathcal{R}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
by

\mathbf{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B}):=\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B}):=\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})\big).

We often abbreviate
\mathbf{D}_{G}(\--)=\mathbf{D}_{G}(\--;\mathcal{B})
and
\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}(\--)=\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}(\--;\mathcal{B})
.

8.
Properties of
\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--)
and
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\--)

8.
a
.
Computation of
\matheurm{D}_{G}(P)

We write again
I\colon\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)\to\mathcal{R}
for the inclusion.

Proposition 8.1
.

There is a zig-zag of equivalences of
\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
-spectra between

I^{*}\Omega\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--))
and
\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{C}_{G}(\--))
.

The proof of Proposition
8.1
will need a preparation.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
P\in\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
. Let
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(P)
be the collection of all subsets
Y
of

|P|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
that are contained in

|P|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\{0,\dots,N\}
for some
N
(depending on
Y
). We
define

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{fin}}}_{G}(P)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}(P)|_{\mathcal{Y}(P)},\mathcal{Y}_{0}(P));

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{sw}}}_{G}(P)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}(P),\mathcal{Y}_{0}(P)).

Here
\mathcal{Y}(P)
is from Definition
7.14
.

As
\matheurm{D}_{G}(P)=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}(P),\mathcal{Y}(P))
and

\mathcal{Y}_{0}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{Y}(P)
we obtain a Karoubi sequence

(8.3)

\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{fin}}}_{G}(P)\;\to\;\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{sw}}}_{G}(P)\;\to\;\matheurm{D}_{G}(P).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
P\in\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
.

(8.4a)

The inclusion

|P|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\to|P|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
,

(\lambda,t)\mapsto(\lambda,0,t)
induces an equivalence

\matheurm{C}_{G}(P)\to\matheurm{D}^{\mathrm{fin}}(P)
;

(8.4b)

The K-theory of
\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{sw}}}_{G}(P)
vanishes.

Proof.

The first statement is an easy exercise in the definitions. The second comes from a
standard Eilenberg swindle on
\matheurm{D}^{{\mathrm{sw}}}_{G}(P)
using the shift

(\lambda,t_{0},t_{1})\mapsto(\lambda,t_{0}+1,t_{1})
; formally we use
Lemma
4.28
.
Let
P\in\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
.
Let
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
be the object whose underlying simplicial complex consist of one vertex which is sent to
P
, i.e.,
{\bf\Sigma}=I(P)
.
Now the point is that for
z,z^{\prime}\in|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}=|P|^{\wedge}
we have for any
\epsilon>0

d_{{\bf\Sigma}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(z,z^{\prime})<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)\quad\iff\quad d_{P\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(z,z^{\prime})<(\beta,\eta).

Thus the conditions in
(7.7b)
are constant in the first coordinate. This
is used to verify
(4.29d)
, the other assumptions of
Lemma
4.28
are straight forward to check.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

The Karoubi sequence (
8.3
) induces a fibration sequence
in K-theory, see (
4.23
). Thus
(8.4a)

and
(8.4b)
give the result.
∎

.

\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--)
on

\mathcal{R}^{0}

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,P)\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. The canonical map

\bigvee_{m\in M}\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{D}_{G}(P(m)))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{D}_{G}(\mathbf{M}))

is an equivalence.

The proof of Proposition
8.5
requires some preparations.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,P)\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. We define the

G
-control structure

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M})=\big(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}_{1}(\mathbf{M}),\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}_{2}(\mathbf{M}),\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}_{G}(\mathbf{M})\big)

as follows. We set
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}_{1}(\mathbf{M}):=\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathbf{M})
and

. We define

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}_{2}(\mathbf{M})
to consist of all

that are

-controlled over

, i.e., satisfy the following. Let

{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime},{\underline{t}}^{\prime}}\\
{\lambda,{\underline{t}}}\end{pmatrix}}\in E
. Write

for the images of

\lambda
and

in

under the projection

. We require

.

Let
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(M)
be the collection of all subsets
Y
of

that are contained in

|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\{0,\dots,N\}

(depending on

).

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{sw}}}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M}),\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\mathbf{M}));

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}_{G}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M}),\mathcal{Y}(\mathbf{M})).

Remark 8.7
.

The
\epsilon
-control aspect of the foliated control condition in
(7.7b)

implies that for all

there is
Y\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbf{M})

such that

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,P)\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
.

(8.8a)

The canonical map

\bigoplus_{m\in M}\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}(P(m))\xrightarrow{\sim}\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathbf{M})

is an equivalence

(8.8b)

The K-theory of

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{sw}}}(\mathbf{M})
vanishes.

(8.8c)

The inclusion

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}(\mathbf{M})
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the compact support condition over
|\Sigma|=M

in
(7.7a)
and the fact that we have
0
-control over over
M
. The
second statement uses the Eilenberg swindle on
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{sw}}}(\mathbf{M})
induced
by the map
(\lambda,t_{0},t_{1})\mapsto(\lambda,t_{0}+1,t_{1})
, see
Lemma
4.28
. To verify

it is again
important that
0
-control over
B
is in
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M})
enforced for all

t_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
43
43

43

Neither
(8.8a)

nor
(8.8b)
hold if we use
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{M})
instead of

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbf{M})
. The third statement is an easy exercise in the

8.7
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

By
(8.8c)
it suffices to prove the assertion for

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}}}
in place of
\matheurm{D}_{G}
. The Karoubi sequence

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathbf{M})\;\to\;\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{sw}}}(\mathbf{M})\;\to\;\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}}}(\mathbf{M})

induces a fibration sequence in K-theory, see (
4.23
).
Using
(8.8b)
we obtain an equivalence

\Omega\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}}}(\--)\big)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}(\--)\big)
. Thus it suffices to prove the assertion with

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{{\mathrm{dis}},{\mathrm{fin}}}
in place of
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{\mathrm{dis}}
. In this formulation the assertion
follows from
(8.8a)
.
∎

8.
c
.
The K-theory of
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})
determines the K-theory of

\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})

Proposition 8.9
.

There exists a diagram in
\mathcal{R}\text{-}\matheurm{Spectra}

\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)}

whose homotopy colimit is equivalent to
\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--)\big)
.

The proof of Proposition
8.9
requires some preparations.

Definition 8.10
.

We define the
G
-control structure

\mathfrak{D}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})=\big(\mathfrak{D}_{1}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma}),\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma}),\mathfrak{D}_{G}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})\big)

as follows. Set
\mathfrak{D}_{1}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma}):=\mathfrak{D}_{1}({\bf\Sigma})
,

\mathfrak{D}_{G}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma}):=\mathfrak{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
. We define

\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})
to consist of all
E\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
satisfying

\qquad{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime},t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{1}}\\
{z,t_{0},t_{1}}\end{pmatrix}}\in E\;\implies\;t_{0}=t^{\prime}_{0}.

We set

\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}({\bf\Sigma}):=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma}),\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})).

Note that

\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})\subseteq\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})\subseteq\mathfrak{D}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
and so

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\subseteq\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\subseteq\matheurm{D}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
.

Lemma 8.11
.

There are homotopy pushouts in
\mathcal{R}\text{-}\matheurm{Spectra}

\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}(\--)\big)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{.5}_{G}(\--)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--)\big).}

We will only give the construction of the right homotopy pushout square in
Lemma
8.11
; the construction of the left homotopy pushout square is
entirely analogous.

Definition 8.12
.

For
X\subseteq\mathbb{N}
let
\mathcal{Y}^{X}({\bf\Sigma})
be the collection of all subsets of

|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times X\times\mathbb{N}
. We define

\matheurm{D}^{X}_{G}({\bf\Sigma}):=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{X}({\bf\Sigma})},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})).

The definition amounts to replacing
|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
with

|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times X\times\mathbb{N}
. Note that because of the bounded control
requirement over
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
in
(7.7b)
the category

\matheurm{D}^{X}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
depends on
X
with the metric it inherits from
\mathbb{N}
. In
particular, the properties of
\matheurm{D}^{X}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
depend on the coarse
structure of
X
.

We now choose natural numbers
0=a_{0}<a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots
such that

a_{n+1}-a_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. We set

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle[a_{0},a_{3}]\cup[a_{4},a_{7}]\cup[a_{\,8},\,a_{11}]\cup[a_{12},a_{15}]\cup\dots;

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle[a_{2},a_{5}]\cup[a_{6},a_{9}]\cup[a_{10},a_{13}]\cup[a_{14},a_{17}]\cup\dots,

where
[a,b]
is the discrete interval
\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
. The point is that each of the
three sets
A
,
B
and
A\cap B
is the infinite union of intervals, where both the
length and the distance between successive grow to
\infty
. We have

(8.13a)

for any
r
there is
R
such that for
a\in A\setminus B

and
b\in B
we have either
a,b\leq R
or
|b-a|\leq r
;

(8.13b)

for any
r
there is
R
such that if

|a_{i}-a_{j}|\leq r
and
a_{i}\neq a_{j}
, then
a_{i},a_{j}\leq R
.

Lemma 8.14
.

The square

\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}^{A\cap B}_{G}(\--)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}(\--)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{B}(\--)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}(\--)\bigr)}

is a homotopy pushout square in
\mathcal{R}\text{-}\matheurm{Spectra}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}
. We check that
Lemma
4.27
applies to the above square evaluated on

{\bf\Sigma}
. Let
Y_{A}\in\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})
,
E\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
be given.
Set
Y_{B}:=Y_{A}\cap\big(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times B\times\mathbb{N}\big)
and

Y^{\prime}_{A}:=Y_{A}\setminus|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times B\times\mathbb{N}
. Then

Y_{A}\in\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})
,
Y_{B}\in\mathcal{Y}^{B}({\bf\Sigma})
and
Y_{A}=Y^{\prime}_{A}\cup Y_{B}
. The
bounded control condition from
(7.7b)
together with
(8.13a)

implies that there is
R>0
such that for all
(z,t_{0},t_{1})\in(Y^{\prime}_{A})^{E}
we have

t_{0}\in A
or
t_{0}\leq R
. Thus
(Y^{\prime}_{A})^{E}\in\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})
and
Lemma
4.27
applies.
∎

Lemma 8.15
.

In
\mathcal{R}\text{-}\matheurm{Spectra}
, the functors

\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}^{A\cap B}_{G}(\--)\bigr)
,
\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}(\--)\bigr)
,

\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{B}(\--)\bigr)
are all equivalent to

\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{.5}(\--)\bigr)
.

Proof.

The argument is almost the same in all three cases. We treat

\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}(\--)\bigr)
. Let
A_{0}:=\{a_{0},a_{4},a_{8},a_{12},\dots\}
. We
claim that for any
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}

(8.15a)

the bijection
\mathbb{N}\to A_{0}
,
i\mapsto a_{4i}
induces an
equivalence
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma})
of categories;

(8.15b)

the inclusion
A_{0}\to A
induces an equivalence

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})
in K-theory.

Clearly,
(8.15a)
and
(8.15b)
together give us an equivalence

\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{.5}(\--)\bigr)\to\mathbf{K}\bigl(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}(\--)\bigr)
.

The difference between
\mathfrak{D}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})
and
\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma})
is that for

{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}}\\
{z,t_{0},t_{1}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}}\in E\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
we can have

t_{0}^{\prime}\neq t_{0}
, while this does not happen for
\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})
. However,
if
t_{0}=a_{i}
and
t^{\prime}_{0}=a_{i^{\prime}}
then
(8.13b)
implies that either

t_{0}=t^{\prime}_{0}
or
t_{0},t^{\prime}_{0}
are bounded. More formally, for

E\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}({\bf\Sigma})
there is
Y\in\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})
such that

(E\setminus Y\times Y)\cap\big(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times A_{0}\times\mathbb{N}\big)^{\times 2}\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{.5}({\bf\Sigma})
and from this it is not difficult to
verify
(8.15a)
.

The Karoubi sequence

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})},\mathcal{Y}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma}))

induce a fibration sequence in K-theory, see (
4.23
). To
prove
(8.15b)
it suffice to show that

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})},\mathcal{Y}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma}))
admits an Eilenberg
swindle. Set
Z:=|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times A_{0}\times\mathbb{N}
. Consider

f\colon|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times A\times\mathbb{N}\to|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times A\times\mathbb{N}
with

f(z,t_{0},t_{1}):=\begin{cases}(z,t_{0},t_{1})&t_{0}\in A_{0}\\
(z,t_{0}-1,t_{1})&t_{0}\not\in A_{0}.\end{cases}

It is not difficult to check that Lemma
4.29
applies and we obtain
a swindle on
\mathcal{B}^{\wedge}_{G}(\mathfrak{E}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})},\mathcal{Y}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma}))
.

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{E}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{A}({\bf\Sigma})},\mathcal{Y}^{A_{0}}({\bf\Sigma}))
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.11
.

The existence of the right hand square follows directly from
Lemma
8.14
and Lemma
8.15
. The left hand
square can be constructed by a similar argument.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.9
.

This follows from Lemma
8.11
.
∎

8.
d
.
Homotopy invariance for
\mathbf{D}_{G}^{0}

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,P)\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. Let

\pi\colon M\times\Delta^{n}\to M
be the projection.
We obtain

{\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}=(M\times\Delta^{d},P\circ\pi_{*})\in\mathcal{R}
as in Subsection
6.e
. A choice of a point

x_{0}\in|\Delta^{d}|
determines an inclusion

\mathbf{i}\colon\mathbf{M}\to{\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Proposition 8.16
.

The inclusion
\mathbf{i}
induces an
equivalence

\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\mathbf{M})\big)\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}})\big).

Proof.

We have
|{\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}|^{\wedge}=|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times|\Delta^{d}|
. Let

\mathcal{Y}(x_{0})
be the collection of all subsets of

|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times\{x\}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\subset|{\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
. Then

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\mathbf{M})=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}(\mathbf{M}),\mathcal{Y}(\mathbf{M}))
is
equivalent to

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}})|_{\mathcal{Y}(x_{0})},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}))
.
We obtain a Karoubi sequence

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}})|_{\mathcal{Y}(x_{0})},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}))\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}))

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Delta}^{d}_{\mathbf{M}})\cup\mathcal{Y}(x_{0}))

as in (
4.23
). It suffices to show that the K-theory of right
most category of this sequence is trivial. To this end we produce an Eilenberg swindle
and use Lemma
4.29
with

Z=|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times\{x_{0}\}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
. The point of the
swindle is that we can contract
|\Delta^{d}|
linearly to
\{x_{0}\}
. The

\epsilon
-control part (see
(7.3a)
) of the foliated control
condition in
(7.7b)
requires a bit of care here: with increasing
t_{0}
we
need to push slower and slower. More precisely, we construct

f\colon|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times|\Delta^{d}|\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}\times|\Delta^{d}|\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
as follows. Write

f_{t_{0}}\colon|\Delta^{d}|\to|\Delta^{d}|
for the map that sends
x
to the point
x^{\prime}

on the straight line from
x
to
x_{0}
in
|\Delta^{d}|
, whose distance from
x
is

\min\{1/t_{0},d(x,x_{0})\}
. We can set

f(\lambda,x,t_{0},t_{1}):=(\lambda,f_{t_{0}}(x),t_{0},t_{1}).

The foliated control condition in
(7.7b)
also involves foliated control
over
|(P\circ\pi_{*})(\{m\}\times\sigma)|^{\wedge}=|P(m)|^{\wedge}

for each
m\in M
,
see
(7.3b)
.
The map
f
preserves this condition,
because
f
acts as the identity on the
|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}
-coordinate and because

|(P\circ\pi_{*})(\{m\}\times\sigma)
depends on
m
but not on
\sigma
(otherwise
the passage from a subsimplex to a larger simplex could create problems). With this
observation it is not difficult to check that the assumptions of
Lemma
4.29
are satisfied.
∎

8.
e
.
Excision for
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)

Let
{\bf\Sigma}=(\Sigma,P)\in\mathcal{R}
be
d
-dimensional. Let

{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{\prime},P^{\prime})
be its
(d\,\text{-}1)
-skeleton. For
W\subseteq|\Sigma|

we let
|W|^{\wedge}
be the preimage of
W
under

|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\xrightarrow{p_{\bf\Sigma}}|\Sigma|
and define
\mathcal{Y}_{W}
as the
collection of all subsets of
|W|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
. We set

\matheurm{D}^{0,W}_{G}({\bf\Sigma}):=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}_{W}},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})),

i.e.,
\matheurm{D}^{0,W}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
is the full subcategory of
\matheurm{D}^{0,W}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
on
all objects whose support is contained in
|W|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
. Setting

\mathcal{Y}_{W}^{+}:=\mathcal{Y}_{W}\cup\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})
, we can apply
Lemma
4.26
to conclude that
\matheurm{D}^{0,W}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
is
canonically equivalent
to
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}^{+}_{W}},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma}))
.

Fix
0<\epsilon<1/(d+1)
. Let
N
be the (open
44
44

44

We could equally well work
with closed neighborhoods.
)
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
|\Sigma^{\prime}|
in
|\Sigma|

(always with respect to the
l^{\infty}
-metric). The choice of
\epsilon
guarantees

|\Sigma^{\prime}|\subseteq N\subsetneq|\Sigma|
.
Let
M
be the complement of the
\epsilon/2
-neighborhood of

|\Sigma^{\prime}|
in
|\Sigma|
. Thus
|\Sigma|=N\cup M
.

Lemma 8.17
.

The functor
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,N}({\bf\Sigma})
induced by the
inclusion
{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime}\to{\bf\Sigma}
yields an equivalence in K-theory.

Proof.

First note that the inclusion
{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime}\to{\bf\Sigma}
induces an equivalence

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,|\Sigma^{\prime}|}({\bf\Sigma})
. We have a Karoubi
sequence

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}_{|\Sigma^{\prime}|}},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma}))\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}_{N}},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma}))

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{N}},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})\cup\mathcal{Y}_{|\Sigma^{\prime}|}))

as in (
4.23
). The first map can be identified with

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,|\Sigma^{\prime}|}({\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,N}({\bf\Sigma})
. Thus we need to show
that the K-theory of the third term is trivial. To this end we will use
Lemma
4.28
to construct an Eilenberg swindle on

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{D}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}(N)},\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})\cup\mathcal{Y}_{|\Sigma^{\prime}|})
. The swindle will be similar to the one constructed in the
proof of homotopy invariance in Proposition
8.16
. This time we
swindle towards
|{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime}|^{\wedge}
in
|N|^{\wedge}
. Let

Z:=|{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
. Let
p\colon N\to|\Sigma^{\prime}|
be the
radial projection, i.e., if
\sigma
is a
d
-simplex of
\Sigma
and

x\in N\cap|\Delta_{\sigma}|
, then
p(x)
is the unique point in

|\partial\Delta_{\sigma}|
such that
x
lies on the straight line between
p(x)
and
the barycenter of
\sigma
. Let
f_{t}(x)
be the point on the straight line from
x
to

p(x)
of distance
\min\{1/(t+1),d(x,p(x))
from
x
. Let

f^{\wedge}_{t}\colon|N|^{\wedge}\to|N|^{\wedge}
be the map that sends

[x,\lambda]_{\sigma}\in|N|^{\wedge}
to
[f_{t}(x),\lambda]_{\sigma}
. We now define

f\colon|N|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to|W|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
by

f(z,t_{0},t_{1})=(f^{\wedge}_{t_{0}},t_{0},t_{1}).

It is not too difficult to check that the assumptions of
Lemma
4.29
are satisfied. As in the proof of
Proposition
8.16
, it is important here that
f_{t}
pushes less
with increasing
t
, in order to preserve the
\epsilon
-control part
(see
(7.3a)
) of the foliated control condition
in
(7.7b)
. As
f_{t}
pushes linearly towards the boundary of

|\Delta_{\sigma}|
, it does preserve the sets
K_{\tau,\epsilon}

from
(7.3b)
. With these observations it is easy to
control the interaction of
f^{\wedge}_{t}
with the foliated control condition
in
(7.7b)
.
∎

Proposition 8.18
.

The diagram

\textstyle{\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,M\cap N}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,M}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,N}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})}

is a homotopy pushout.

Proof.

It is not difficult to check that Lemma
4.27

applies, where we use
\mathcal{Y}_{0}:=\mathcal{Y}_{N}^{+}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{1}:=\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{+}
and observe that

\mathcal{Y}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{M\cap N}
and that, as
|\Sigma|=M\cup N
,

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M\cup N}}=\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})
. To verify that the
assumption from Lemma
4.25
(as required in
Lemma
4.27
) is satisfied, observe that the distance
between points in
M\setminus N
and in
N\setminus M
is uniformly bounded from below
by some
\delta>0
. Let
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{+}
and
E\in\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})
be
given. The
\epsilon
-control requirement in
(7.7b)
for
E
gives us
some
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime},t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{1}}\\
{z,t_{0},t_{1}}\end{pmatrix}}\in E
with

t_{0}=t^{\prime}_{0}\geq k_{0}
, then the distance of
p_{\bf\Sigma}(z)
and
p_{\bf\Sigma}(z^{\prime})
in

|\Sigma|
is
<\delta
; in particular
p_{\bf\Sigma}(z)
and
p_{\bf\Sigma}(z^{\prime})
are either
both in
M
or in
N
. Then with

Y_{0}:=|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}_{\leq k_{0}}\in\mathcal{Y}({\bf\Sigma})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{N}^{+}
,
Y^{\prime}_{1}:=Y_{1}\setminus Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{+}
we have
(Y^{\prime}_{1})^{E}\in\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{+}
.
∎

Let now
\widehat{\bf\Sigma}=(\widehat{\Sigma},\widehat{P})
be as in
Subsection
6.f
, i.e.,
\widehat{\Sigma}=B\times\Delta^{d}
, where

B
is the set of
d
-simplices of
\Sigma
. We can also apply the previous definitions
to
\widehat{\bf\Sigma}
and obtain
\widehat{N}
,

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0,\widehat{M}}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})
and so on.

Lemma 8.19
.

The functors

\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}\cap\widehat{N}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}^{0,M\cap N}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\quad\text{and}\quad\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\to\matheurm{D}^{0,M}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})

induced by the projection
\widehat{\bf\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{f}}{\bf\Sigma}
are
equivalences.

Proof.

The projection
|\widehat{\Sigma}|\xrightarrow{f}\Sigma|
restricts to an bijection

\widehat{M}\to M
. The restrictions of the
l^{\infty}
-metrics of
|\widehat{\Sigma}|

and
|\Sigma|
to
\widehat{M}
and
M
are different, but agree on path components.
Moreover, in both cases the distance between points in different path components is
bounded from below by a universal constant (depending only on
d
). With these
observations it is not difficult to verify the assertion.
∎

Proposition 8.20
.

The diagram

(8.21)

\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\iota}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{f}^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{f}_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\big)}

obtained by applying K-theory to (
6.6
) is a homotopy
pushout diagram.

Proof.

Consider

\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}\cap\widehat{N}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1)}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}\cap\widehat{N}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(3)}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{M}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{N}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(2)}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,M\cap N}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(4)}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,M}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,N}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0,N}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})}

We will argue that (2) is a homotopy pushout in K-theory. This will give the assertion
of the proposition, as Lemma
8.17
(which also applies to

\widehat{\bf\Sigma}
) allows us to replace
\matheurm{D}^{0,\widehat{N}}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma})

with
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\widehat{\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})
and
\matheurm{D}^{0,N}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})
with

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma}^{\prime})
. (Note that the positions of the right/top and left/bottom
corners in (
8.21
) and in (2) are switched.)

Proposition
8.18
(which also applies to
\widehat{\bf\Sigma}
) tells us that (1)
and (4) are homotopy pushouts in K-theory. Lemma
8.19
implies that (3)
is a homotopy pushouts in K-theory as well. As the combinations of (1) with (2) and
(3) with (4) agree, this implies that (4) is homotopy pushouts in K-theory.
∎

8.
f
.
Skeleton continuity of
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)

Proposition 8.22
.

For any
{\bf\Sigma}\in\mathcal{R}
the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}^{d})\big)\;\to\;\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})\big)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

The finite dimensional support condition in Definition
7.7
directly
implies that
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma})
is the directed union of the

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}({\bf\Sigma}^{d})
and this gives the result.
∎

9.
Outline of the construction of the transfer

We now assume that
\mathcal{B}
is a Hecke category with
G
-support.
As before we abbreviate
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma})=\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}({\bf\Sigma};\mathcal{B})
.
Theorem
6.7
asserts that the maps

(9.1)

\mathbf{K}\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}\big(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)\times\mathbf{M}\big)\;\xrightarrow{\mathbf{p}_{M}}\;\mathbf{K}\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\mathbf{M})

induced by the projections

\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)\times\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
admit sections
\mathbf{tr}_{\mathbf{M}}
that are natural in
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. In
this outline we will concentrate on the case where
\mathbf{M}=\ast
is the terminal
object
45
45

45

i.e.,
\ast=(\Delta^{0},\ast_{\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)})
.
in
\mathcal{R}
; the
general case requires no real additional input.

9.
a
.
Sequences

To construct a section
to (

See Appendix

.

functor

\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\ast)\to\ch_{\mathrm{fin}}\Idem\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}\big(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)\big).

We use the sequence description of

{\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\textstyle\prod}}_{\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\mathcal{B}_{G}(|{\bf\Sigma}|^{\wedge})

(7.18a)
,
(7.18b)
,
(7.18c)

(7.18d)

spelled out in

Objects in
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\ast)

\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
of objects in

{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}

Morphisms in
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\ast)
are (equivalence classes of) sequences of morphisms

\varphi=(\varphi_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)
such that
\supp_{G}\varphi_{\underline{t}}\subseteq K

for all
{\underline{t}}
for some compact subset

{\underline{t}}
. In order for

from

. This means roughly the following. Given a compact subset

of

we need
\beta>0
,
\eta_{\underline{t}},\epsilon_{\underline{t}}>0

as

(for fixed

) and
\epsilon_{{\underline{t}}}\to 0
as

(uniform in

), such that if
\varphi
is a morphism in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)
with
\supp_{G}\varphi\subseteq K
, then

\qquad{\begin{pmatrix}{z^{\prime}}\\
{z}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\tilde{F}_{{\underline{t}}}(\varphi)\;\implies\;d_{{\bf\Sigma}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(z,z^{\prime})<(\beta,\eta_{\underline{t}},\epsilon_{\underline{t}}).

These two required properties, finiteness for objects,
and foliated control for morphisms,
are in tension with each other
47
47

47

For example, it is not difficult to construct a
functor

that for
{\begin{pmatrix}{z}\\
{z}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\tilde{F}_{t}(\varphi_{t})

we
even have

, but such an

will fail the required finiteness property for
objects, see Remark
11.11
.
. Let
X
be the extended Bruhat-Tits
building associated to
G
. The functors
\tilde{F}_{\underline{t}}
are constructed as
a composition

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)\;\xrightarrow{F_{\underline{t}}}\;\ch_{\mathrm{fin}}\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(X)\;\xrightarrow{(f_{\underline{t}})_{*}}\;\ch_{\mathrm{fin}}\big(\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)|^{\wedge})\big)

where the

are given by a tensor product with certain complexes over

and

give a brief outline for both below.

9.
b
.
The diagonal tensor product

Given two smooth
G
-representations
V
,
W
we can equip
V\otimes W
with the diagonal
action
g\cdot(v\otimes w)=gv\otimes gw
. We obtain a functor

V\otimes\--\;\colon\;\matheurm{Rep}(G)\;\to\;\matheurm{Rep}(G)

on categories of smooth representations. If the underlying vector space of
V
is finite
dimensional, then this functor preserves finitely generated and projective
representations. If
V
is the permutation representation of a smooth
G
-set
\Sigma
,
then for Hecke category
\mathcal{B}
with
G
-suport this can be generalized
48
48

48

The
formula in Subsection
11.c
looks a priori different. The
translation between the two functors uses a shearing isomorphism.
to

\Sigma\otimes\--\;\colon\;\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)\;\to\;\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast).

Moreover, a map
c\colon\Sigma\to X
determines a lift of this functor to

(\Sigma,c)\otimes\--\;\colon\;\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)\;\to\;\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(X).

In Subsection
10.a
we define a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear category
\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
, whose
objects are pairs
(\Sigma,c)
as above, and in
Subsection
11.c
we obtain a functor

\--\otimes\--\;\colon\;\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)\times\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)\;\to\;\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(X).

The functor
(\Sigma,c)\otimes\--
will provide a splitting for

\mathcal{B}_{G}(X)\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)
if
\Sigma=G/G
, but
this does not give us enough flexibility to construct a sequence
F_{t}
that will satisfy
the foliated control condition from
(7.7b)
. We can view the singular chain
complex
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
of
X
as a chain complex over
\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
49
49

49

For technical
reason we will have to replace
X
with the set
S(X)
of singular simplices in
X
and
view
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
as a chain complex over
\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
; applying the barycenter map

S(X)\to X
we then obtain a chain complex over
\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
.
, see
Subsection
10.b
, and obtain

\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\otimes\--\;\colon\;\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)\;\to\;\ch\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(X).

As
X
is contractible this functor is much closer to providing a splitting and is much
better compatible with control conditions for morphisms,
see
(11.9c)
. The remaining problem is that the singular
chain complex is very large and this will lead to conflict with the finiteness
conditions
(7.7a)
, see Remark
11.11
. Of
course, as
X
is contractible,
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
is finite up to homotopy. But such a homotopy
involves moving through
X
and incorporating it into our construction would again lead to
conflict with the foliated control condition from
(7.7b)
. The solution is
a compromise between
X
and a point. We will use large balls
B_{\underline{t}}
in

X
. Moreover, in place of the singular complex we will use the simplicial complex of a
suitable (fine) triangulation of
B_{\underline{t}}
. The balls
B_{\underline{t}}

are not
G
-invariant. To resolve this we use that
B_{\underline{t}}\subseteq X
is
a deformation retract via the radial projection
X\to B_{\underline{t}}
. This way

B_{\underline{t}}
inherits a homotopy coherent
G
-action from the
G
-action on
X
.
Altogether we will construct a homotopy coherent functor

F_{\underline{t}}\colon\mathcal{B}_{G,U_{\underline{t}}}(\ast)\;\to\;\ch_{\mathrm{fin}}\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(X),

see Subsection
11.g
. Here

U_{\underline{t}}
is the compact open subgroup of
G
that fixes the ball

B_{\underline{t}}
pointwise and

\mathcal{B}_{G,U_{\underline{t}}}(\ast)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)
is the full subcategory on objects
(S,\pi,B)
where

\supp B(s)\subseteq U_{t}
for all
s
. Passing to this subcategory is not a serious
restriction, by the support cofinality property
(3.2d)
for

\mathcal{B}
, the idempotent completions of

\mathcal{B}_{G,U_{\underline{t}}}(\ast)
and
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\ast)

coincide. The deformation of
X
onto
B_{\underline{t}}
still moves through
X
,
creating conflict with the foliated control condition from
(7.7b)
. We will
outline how this conflict is resolved in the next subsection.

9.
c
.
The maps
X\to|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)|^{\wedge}

Recall that morphisms in
\matheurm{D}_{G}^{0}(\ast)

-support. Using

50

50

Typically the

.
. This leads to the following requirements for the maps

There is third requirement

for

]
.

10.
The category
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)

Throughout this section we fix a smooth
G
-space
X
.
We assume that for

K\subseteq X
compact, the pointwise isotropy group
G_{K}=\bigcap_{x\in K}G_{x}
is an
open subgroup of
G
.
Later
X
will be the extended Bruhat-Tits building associated to a reductive

p
-adic group.

10.
a
.
The category
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)

Definition 10.1
.

For a smooth
G
-set
\Omega
we define the additive category
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
as
follows. Objects are pairs
\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)
where
\Sigma
is a smooth
G
-set and

c\colon\Sigma\to\Omega
is a
G
-map. A morphism

\rho\colon\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})
is an

\Sigma\times\Sigma^{\prime}
-matrix

(\rho_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma\in\Sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}}
over
\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
the following two conditions

(10.1a)

for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
the set

\{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}\mid\rho_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\neq 0\}
is finite;

(10.1b)

for all
g\in G
,
\sigma\in\Sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
we have

\rho_{g\sigma}^{g\sigma^{\prime}}=\rho_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

The support of
\rho
is

\supp_{2}\rho:=\Big\{{\begin{pmatrix}{c^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})}\\
{c(\sigma)}\end{pmatrix}}\,\Big|\,\rho_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\neq 0\Big\}\subseteq\Omega\times\Omega.

The support of
\mathbf{V}
is
\supp_{1}\mathbf{V}:=c(\Sigma)
.
Composition is matrix multiplication

(\rho^{\prime}\circ\rho)_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}:=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}\circ\rho_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
.
We will say that
\mathbf{V}
is finite, if
\Sigma
is finite.

The identity
\id_{\mathbf{V}}
of
\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)
is given by

(\id_{\mathbf{V}})_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=1
for
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and

(\id_{\mathbf{V}})_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=0
for
\sigma\not=\sigma^{\prime}
.

10.
b
.
The singular chain complex of
X
as a chain complex over
\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))

Let
S_{n}(X)
be the set of singular
n
-simplices of
X
.
Let
S(X)
be the union of the
S_{n}(X)
.
By our assumption on
X
this is a smooth
G
-set via the
G
-action on
X
.
Let
c_{n}\colon S_{n}(X)\to S(X)
be the inclusion.
We obtain

(S_{n}(X),c_{n})\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
.
We define
\partial_{n}\colon(S_{n}(X),c_{n})\to(S_{n-1}(X),c_{n-1})
by

(10.2)

(\partial_{n})_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}(-1)^{i}&\text{if $\sigma^{\prime}$ is the $i$-th face of $\sigma$;}\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

We write
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\in\ch\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
for the chain complex in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
obtained this way.

Later we will use the
G
-map
\bary\colon S(X)\to X
that sends

\sigma\colon|\Delta^{n}|\to X
to the image of the barycenter of
|\Delta^{n}|
under

\sigma
. Then
\bary_{*}(\mathbf{S}_{*}(X))\in\ch\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
51
51

51

A
G
-map
f\colon\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
induces a map
f_{*}\colon\ch\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\to\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega^{\prime})
by composition.
.
Working in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
and
not in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
will allow us to consider certain restrictions in
Subsection
12.d
.

10.
c
.
Subspace

For some purposes the singular chain complex
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
is too big. To replace it by
smaller chain complexes we will pass from
X
to a subspace (and later to a subspace
equipped with a triangulation). Typically, the subspace will not be
G
-invariant, and we
will have to pass to an open subgroup as well.

Let
U\subseteq G
be an open subgroup. Let
D\subseteq X
be a
U
-invariant
subspace
52
52

52

Later on
D
will be contained in the
U
-fixed point set
X^{U}
. We
write
S_{n}(D)
for the set of singular simplices of
D
. We obtain the chain complex

\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)
over
\mathcal{S}^{U}(S(X))
whose
n
-th chain module is
(S_{n}D,c_{n}|_{S_{n}(D)})
. The
boundary map is still given by (
10.2
).

Let
D^{\prime}
be a further
U
-invariant subspace. A
U
-equivariant map

f\colon D\to D^{\prime}
induces a chain map

f_{*}\colon\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)\to\mathbf{S}_{*}(D^{\prime})
in
\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(S(X))
with

(f_{*})_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;\sigma^{\prime}=f\circ\sigma;\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

A
U
-equivariant homotopy
H\colon D\times[0,1]\to D^{\prime}
with
H(-,0)=f_{0}
,

H(-,1)=f_{1}
determines a chain homotopy
H_{*}\colon(f_{0})_{*}\simeq(f_{1})_{*}
by the
usual formula. In order to give the formula in detail, we write
v_{k}
for the
k
-th
vertex of
\Delta^{n}
and let
i_{j}\colon|\Delta^{n+1}|\to|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]
be
the affine map determined by

i_{j}(v_{k}):=\begin{cases}(v_{k},0)&j\leq k;\\
(v_{k-1},1)&j>k.\end{cases}

Then
53
53

53

Usually
H_{*}
is not written as a matrix; for this reason the formula may not look
familiar at a first glance.

(H_{*})_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=\sum_{j\colon\sigma^{\prime}=H\circ(\sigma\times\id_{[0,1]})\circ i_{j}}(-1)^{j}.

Lemma 10.3
.

For
H\colon D\times[0,1]\to D^{\prime}
we have

{\begin{pmatrix}{\sigma^{\prime}}\\
{\sigma}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}H_{*}\quad\implies\quad\Image(\sigma^{\prime})\subseteq\Image(H\circ\sigma\times\id_{[0,1]}).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the formula for
H_{*}
reviewed above.
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
H\colon D\times[0,1]\to D^{\prime}
be a homotopy between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
.
Let
d_{X}
be a metric on
X
.
Assume that
D
is compact.
Assume that for all
s\in[0,1]
and all
x,x^{\prime}\in B
we have

d_{X}\big(H(x,s),H(x^{\prime},s)\big)\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime}).

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then there is a chain homotopy

\widetilde{H}\colon\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)\to\mathbf{S}_{*+1}(D^{\prime})
between
(f_{0})_{*}
and

(f_{1})_{*}
with the following property. Let
{\begin{pmatrix}{\sigma^{\prime}}\\
{\sigma}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\widetilde{H}
54
54

54

The support of a graded map is the union of the supports of the maps in all degrees.
. Then

(a)

if the diameter of the image of
\sigma
is
<\kappa
, then the diameter of the
image of
\sigma^{\prime}
is
<\kappa+\epsilon
;

(b)

\Image\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Image(H\circ\sigma\times\id_{[0,1]})
.

Proof.

As
D
is compact we can find
\delta>0
such that

d_{X}(H(x,s),H(x,s^{\prime}))<\epsilon/2
for all
x\in D
and all
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,1]
with

|s-s^{\prime}|<\delta
. Now we choose
0=s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}=1
with

|s_{i+1}-s_{i}|<\delta
. Then

\widetilde{H}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(H|_{D\times[s_{i-1},s_{i}]})_{*}
is a chain homotopy between
(f_{0})_{*}
and

(f_{1})_{*}
. Now

\supp\widetilde{H}_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\supp\big((H|_{D\times[s_{i-1},s_{i}]})_{*}\big)
and it is easy to check that
\widetilde{H}
has the required properties.
∎

10.
d
.
Triangulations

Assume that the
U
-invariant subspace
D
of
X
is equipped with a
U
-invariant
triangulation, i.e.,
D=|K|
for a simplicial complex
K
with a smooth
U
-action.
Assume that the triangulation is locally ordered, i.e., there is a partial order on the
set of vertices that restricts to a linear order on the vertex set of every simplex.
This ensures that every simplex in the triangulation determines a unique singular simplex
of
D
and therefore of
X
. In particular, we can view the set
\simp_{n}(D)
of

n
-simplices of
D
as a subset of
S_{n}D
. We obtain the chain complex

\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)\in\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}S(X))
, whose
n
-th chain module is

(\simp_{n}(D),c_{n}|_{\simp_{n}(D)})
. It comes with an inclusion

i\colon\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)\to\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)
defined by

i_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma;\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Let
d_{X}
be a metric on
X
. For a singular simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{n}\to X
we
write
\Image\sigma
for its image and
(\Image\sigma)^{\epsilon}
for the open

\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\Image\sigma
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Assume that all simplices of the
triangulation of
D
are of diameter
<\epsilon
. In
\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(S(X))
there
exists a chain map
r\colon\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)\to\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)
with

r\circ i=\id_{\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)}
and a chain homotopy

H\colon\id_{\mathbf{S}_{*}(D)}\simeq i\circ r
such that if

{\begin{pmatrix}{\sigma^{\prime}}\\
{\sigma}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}r\cup\supp_{2}H
, then

\Image\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq(\Image\sigma)^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

This a minor variation of
[
9
, Lem. 6.9]
. Let
\mathcal{K}

be the poset of subcomplexes of
K
ordered by inclusion. We view
\mathcal{K}
as a category
and work now in the abelian category of
{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{K}}
-modules
55
55

55

That is in the
category of functors
\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod}
.
. For
K_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
let

\underline{C}_{*}(K_{0})
be the simplicial chain complex of
K_{0}
and

\underline{S}_{*}(K_{0})
be the singular chain complex of
|K_{0}|
. This defines chain
complexes
\underline{C}_{*}
and
\underline{S}_{*}
of
{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{K}}
-modules and it is not
difficult to check that the underlying
{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{K}}
-modules
\underline{C}_{n}
,

\underline{S}_{n}
are free (and thus projective) for all
n
.
Write

\underline{i}\colon\underline{C}_{*}\to\underline{S}_{*}
for the inclusion. Each

\underline{i}_{n}\colon\underline{C}_{n}\to\underline{S}_{n}
is the inclusion of a
direct summand. Moreover,
\underline{i}
induces an isomorphism in homology (taken in
the category of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{K}
-modules). By general results in homological algebra (in
abelian categories) it follows that there exists a chain map

\underline{r}\colon\underline{S}_{*}\to\underline{C}_{*}
with

\underline{r}\circ\underline{i}=\id_{C_{*}}
and a homotopy

\underline{H}\colon\id_{S_{*}}\simeq i\circ r
. We can set
r:=\underline{r}(K)
,

H:=\underline{H}(K)
. The additional properties of
r
and
H
follow from the
functoriality in
\mathcal{K}
of
\underline{r}
and
\underline{H}
: Suppose

r_{\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\neq 0
. Let
K_{\sigma}
be the smallest subcomplex of
K
such that

|K_{\sigma}|
contains
\Image\sigma
, so
\sigma\in\underline{S}_{*}(K_{\sigma})
. As

\underline{r}(K_{\sigma})\colon\underline{S}_{*}(K_{\sigma})\to\underline{C}_{*}(K_{\sigma})

it follows that
\sigma^{\prime}
is a simplex of
K_{\sigma}
. Now

|K_{\sigma}|\subseteq(\Image\sigma)^{\epsilon}
as the diameter of simplices in
K
are
of diameter
<\epsilon
. Thus
\Image\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq(\Image\sigma)^{\epsilon}
.
The same argument applies to
H
.
∎

11.
Diagonal tensor products

Throughout this section we fix a
G
-set
\Lambda
and a smooth
G
-space
X
. Later we
will have
\Lambda=|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}
for
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
, while for
X
we will take
the extended Bruhat-Tits building associated to a reductive
p
-adic group. We also fix a
smooth
G
-set
\Omega
. Later
\Omega=S(X)
will be the set of singular simplices of

X
.

To simplify the discussion we assume throughout this section that
\mathcal{B}
is the category

\mathcal{B}(G;R)
from Example
3.3
. This means in particular that if

\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)
is an object from
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
, then the
B(s)

for
s\in S
are compact open subgroups of
G
. Also, if

\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\to(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is a morphism in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
, then the
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}
are elements of the Hecke
algebra
\mathcal{H}(G;R)
satisfying
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(a^{\prime}ga)=\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)
for all

a\in B(s)
,
a^{\prime}\in B(s^{\prime})
.

Everything
56
56

56

More precisely, there is a diagonal tensor product such that
Lemmas
11.7
, and
11.9
, and the
identities (
11.12
), (
11.13
) are still
valid, see
[
7
, Lem. 7.41, 7.43, 7.44]
. Everything else in this section just depends on these results.
we need also
works for general Hecke categories with
G
-support and is treated in detail
in
[
7
, Sec. 7]
.

11.
a
.
Precursor

Our first goal in this section is the construction of a bilinear functor

\--\otimes\--\;\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda).

The construction is easier under some simplifying assumptions.
So we assume for this subsection
that
G
is discrete and consider the full subcategory

\mathcal{B}^{1}_{G}(\Lambda)
of
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
on the
objects
(S,\pi,B)
, for which
B(s)
is the trivial subgroup for all
s
.
For

\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
and

\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\in\mathcal{B}^{1}_{G}(\Lambda)
, we can then define

(11.1)

\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{B}:=(\Sigma\times S,c\times\pi,(\sigma,s)\mapsto B(s)).

For
\rho\colon(\Sigma,c)\to(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})
and

\varphi\colon(S,\pi,B)\to(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},B^{\prime})
we can define

(11.2)

(\rho\otimes\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(g):=\rho_{g\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g).

The general idea is that we use the
G
-action on
\Sigma
to twist morphisms in

\mathcal{B}^{1}_{G}(\Lambda)
57
57

57

The category

\mathcal{B}^{1}_{G}(\Lambda)
is equivalent to the category of free

R[G]
-modules. Under this equivalence our functor is equivalent to

((\Sigma,c),M)\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M
, where the tensor product over

\mathbb{Z}
is equipped with the diagonal action of
G
.
. In the general case these formulas
do not define a functor; for example we can have

(\rho\otimes\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}\neq(\rho\otimes\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(a^{\prime}ga)
for
a^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}(s^{\prime})
,
a\in B(s)
. To account for
this we will replace
(\sigma,s)\mapsto B(s)
with

(\sigma,s)\mapsto G_{\sigma}\cap B(s)
. The only remaining drawback is that the functor
obtained this way does not preserve units. We will correct this using the idempotent
completion.

Remark 11.3

(Non-unital categories)
.

In this remark we will contemplate categories without units. Let

\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}:=\underline{\mathcal{H}(G;R)}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear category with exactly one
object whose endomorphism ring is the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G;R)
. As
\mathcal{H}(G;R)
does
not have a unit,
\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}
is a non-unital category, but its idempotent completion

\Idem\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}
has units. Via
U\mapsto\frac{\chi_{U}}{\mu(U)}
the category

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(G;R)
can be identified with a full subcategory of
\Idem\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}
.
Definition
4.1
also makes sense for
\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}
in place of

\mathcal{B}
and we obtain the non-unital category
\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(X)
. Now the
formulas (
11.1
) and (
11.2
) define a
functor (of non-unital categories)

\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda).

Of course this functor sends idempotents to idempotents and so induces a functor (of
unital categories)

\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\Idem\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\Idem\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda).

One can now identify
\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Omega)
with a full subcategory of

\Idem\mathcal{B}^{\text{\tiny nu}}_{G}(\Omega)
and use this to obtain

\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda).

This is essentially what we will do in Subsections
11.b

and
11.c
, although we will avoid non-unital categories and
instead give the resulting formulas more directly.

11.
b
.
The diagonal tensor product
\otimes_{0}

We define

\--\otimes_{0}\--\;\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda)

as follows. For
\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
and

\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\in\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
we set

\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}:=(\Sigma\times S,c\times\pi,(\sigma,s)\mapsto G_{\sigma}\cap B(s)).

For morphisms
\rho\colon\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)

and
\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
we define

(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{s,^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{s,\sigma}(g):=\rho_{g\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)

as in (
11.2
).

We will check in Lemmas
11.4
and
11.5
below
that
\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi
is well defined and compatible with composition. While we
do not claim that
\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{0}\id_{\mathbf{B}}
is
\id_{\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}}
,
compatibility with composition implies that
\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{0}\id_{\mathbf{B}}
is an
idempotent endomorphism of
\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}
.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
\rho\colon\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and

\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
. Then

\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi\colon\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
is a
morphism in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda)

Proof.

For
a\in G_{\sigma}\cap B(s)
,
a^{\prime}\in G_{\sigma^{\prime}}\cap B(s^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(a^{\prime}ga)=\rho_{a^{\prime}ga\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(a^{\prime}ga)=

\displaystyle\rho_{g\sigma}^{(a^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{g\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)=(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(g),

so for fixed
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}
,

(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}\colon G_{\sigma}\cap B(s)\to G_{\sigma^{\prime}}\cap B(s^{\prime})
is a morphism in
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(G;R)
. We also need to check
that
\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi
is column finite. Fix
(\sigma,s)
. We need to check
that there are only finitely many
(\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime})
with

(\rho\otimes\varphi)_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\neq 0
. As
\varphi
is column finite
there is
S^{\prime}_{0}\subset S^{\prime}
finite such that
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}\neq 0
implies

s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{0}
. The
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}
are compactly supported. Thus there is

M\subseteq G
compact such that
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)\neq 0
implies
g\in M
. As

\Sigma
is a smooth
G
-set, the set
M\cdot\sigma\subseteq\Sigma
is finite. As

\rho
is column finite there is
\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
finite such that

\rho_{g\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\neq 0
with
g\in M
implies
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}
. Now
if
(\rho\otimes\varphi)_{s,\sigma}^{s^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\neq 0
, then for some
g\in G
we
have
\rho^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{g\sigma}\neq 0
and
\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)\neq 0
. Thus

(\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}\times S^{\prime}_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
(S,\pi)\xrightarrow{\varphi}(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\varphi^{\prime}}(S^{\prime\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
be
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
and

(\Sigma,c)\xrightarrow{\rho}(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\rho^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime})
be
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
. Then

(\rho^{\prime}\otimes_{0}\varphi^{\prime})\circ(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)=(\rho^{\prime}\circ\rho)\otimes_{0}(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\varphi).

Proof.

\displaystyle\big((\rho^{\prime}\otimes_{0}\varphi^{\prime})\circ(\rho\otimes_{0}

\displaystyle\varphi)\big)_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}}(g)\;\;=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\Big((\rho^{\prime}\otimes_{0}\varphi^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\circ(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}\Big)(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\int_{x\in G}(\rho^{\prime}\otimes_{0}\varphi^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(gx)\circ(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\int_{x\in G}(\rho^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{gx\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(gx)\cdot\rho^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{x^{-1}\sigma}\cdot\varphi^{s^{\prime}}_{s}(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\int_{x\in G}(\rho^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{gx\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\rho^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{x^{-1}\sigma}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(gx)\cdot\varphi^{s^{\prime}}_{s}(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\int_{x\in G}(\rho^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{gx\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\rho^{gx\sigma^{\prime}}_{g\sigma}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(gx)\cdot\varphi^{s^{\prime}}_{s}(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime},s\in S^{\prime}}\int_{x\in G}(\rho^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\rho^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{g\sigma}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(gx)\cdot\varphi^{s^{\prime}}_{s}(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\rho^{\prime}\circ\rho)^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{g\sigma}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\varphi)^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{s}(g)

\displaystyle=\big((\rho^{\prime}\circ\rho)\otimes_{0}(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\varphi)\big)^{\sigma^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma,s}(g).

∎

11.
c
.
The diagonal tensor product
\otimes

For
\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
we set

\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{B}:=(\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B},\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{0}\id_{\mathbf{B}}).

For morphisms

\rho\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(X)
and

\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
we define

(\rho\otimes\varphi):=(\rho\otimes_{0}\varphi)\colon\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{B}^{\prime}.

Now
\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\id_{\mathbf{B}}=\id_{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{B}}
.
Altogether we have now defined a bilinear functor

(11.6)

\--\otimes\--\;\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)\to\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda).

The following observation will often allow us to get rid of idempotent completions.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\in\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
. If

\Sigma
is fixed pointwise by all
B(s)
, then
\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}
.

Proof.

The content of the lemma is that
\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{0}\id_{\mathbf{B}}
is the identity of

\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}
, not just an idempotent. Indeed, since

G_{\sigma}\cap B(s)=B(s)
for all
\sigma
and
s
we have

(\id_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{0}\id_{\mathbf{B}})_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(g)=(\id_{\mathbf{V}})_{g\sigma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(\id_{\mathbf{B}})_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)=(\id_{\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}})_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(g).

∎

For
E\subseteq\Omega\times\Omega
and
E^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda\times\Lambda

we use the following convention

(11.8)

E\times E^{\prime}:=\Big\{{\begin{pmatrix}{x^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{x,\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\;\Big|\;{\begin{pmatrix}{x^{\prime}}\\
{x}\end{pmatrix}}\in E,{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\in E^{\prime}\Big\}\subseteq(\Omega\times\Lambda)^{\times 2}.

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\in\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
.

(11.9a)

If
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{B}
are finite,
then
\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B}
is finite as well;

(11.9b)

\supp_{1}(\mathbf{V}\otimes_{0}\mathbf{B})=\supp_{1}\mathbf{V}\times\supp_{1}\mathbf{B}
.

Let
\rho\colon\mathbf{V}=(\Sigma,c)\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=(\Sigma^{\prime},c^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
,

\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
. Then
for
\rho\otimes\varphi
in
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda)
we have

(11.9c)

\supp_{2}(\rho\otimes\varphi)\;\subseteq\;\supp_{2}\rho\times\supp_{2}\varphi
;

(11.9d)

\supp_{G}(\rho\otimes\varphi)\;\subseteq\;\supp_{G}\varphi
.

Proof.

These claims are straight forward from the definitions.
We give some details for
(11.9c)
.
Let
{\begin{pmatrix}{x^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{x,\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}(\rho\otimes\varphi)
. Then there are

\sigma\in\Sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
,
s\in S
,
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
,
g\in G
with

(\rho\otimes\varphi)_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(g)\neq 0
and
x=gc(\sigma)
,

x^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})
,
\lambda=g\pi(s)
,
\lambda^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime})
. By definition

(\rho\otimes\varphi)_{\sigma,s}^{\sigma^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(g)=\rho^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{g\sigma}\cdot\varphi_{s}^{s^{\prime}}(g)
and thus

{\begin{pmatrix}{x^{\prime}}\\
{x}\end{pmatrix}}={\begin{pmatrix}{c^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})}\\
{gc(\sigma)}\end{pmatrix}}={\begin{pmatrix}{c^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})}\\
{c(g\sigma)}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\rho
and

{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}={\begin{pmatrix}{\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime})}\\
{g\pi(s)}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\varphi
.
∎

11.
d
.
Tensor product with a singular chain complex

Consider the singular chain complex
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\in\ch\mathcal{S}^{G}(S(X))
from
Subsection
10.b
. Using the diagonal tensor
product (
11.6
) we obtain a functor

\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\,\otimes\;\--\;\colon\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)\,\to\,\ch\Idem\big(\mathcal{B}_{G}(S(X)\times\Lambda)\big).

Note that for a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
in

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
by
(11.9c)
we have

(11.10)

\supp_{2}\big(\id_{\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)}\otimes\varphi\big)\subseteq\Big\{{\begin{pmatrix}{\sigma,\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{\sigma,\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\,\Big|\,\sigma\in S(X),{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime}}\\
{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\in\supp_{2}\varphi\Big\}.

Remark 11.11

(Shortcomings of
\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
)
.

Let
J:=|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)|^{\wedge}
,

\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
, and
\Lambda:=|\mathbf{M}|^{\wedge}
. Let

f\colon S(X)\to|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)|
be a
G
-equivariant map. Suppose also that

\widehat{f}\colon S(X)\to|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)|^{\wedge}
is a lift of
f
. In light
of (
11.10
) one might hope, that

(\widehat{f}\times\id_{|P|^{\wedge}})_{*}(\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\otimes-)
induces a
functor
58
58

58

We use the sequence description of
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\--)
from
Remark
7.17
.

\displaystyle\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(P)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\ch\Idem\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}\big(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)\times P\big),

\displaystyle(\mathbf{B}_{{\underline{t}}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\big((\widehat{f}\times\id_{|P|^{\wedge}})_{*}(\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\otimes\mathbf{B}_{{\underline{t}}})\big)_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}},

but this is not the case. Let

(\mathbf{B}_{{\underline{t}}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}=(S_{\underline{t}},\pi_{\underline{t}},B_{\underline{t}})_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}\in\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(P)
. Typically

\big((\widehat{f}\times\id_{|P|^{\wedge}})_{*}(\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}})\big)_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
will fail in two
ways to define a chain complex in
\Idem\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(P\times\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}})
.

Firstly, the boundary maps

(\widehat{f}\times\id_{|P|^{\wedge}})_{*}(\partial_{n}\otimes\id_{\mathbf{B}_{t}})
do not satisfy
the required control conditions to define morphisms in

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)\times P)
. For example the
\epsilon
-control condition over

|J_{\mathcal{F}}|
from Remark
7.9
will typically fail. The
support of

(\widehat{f}\times\id_{P})_{*}(\partial_{n}\otimes\id_{\mathbf{B}_{\underline{t}}})
is the
set of all

{\begin{pmatrix}{\widehat{f}(x^{\prime}),\lambda}\\
{g\widehat{f}(x),g\lambda}\end{pmatrix}}\in(|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}|^{\wedge}\times|P|^{\wedge})^{\times 2}

with
g\in\supp_{G}B_{\underline{t}}(s)
for some
s\in S
with

\pi_{\underline{t}}(s)=x
the barycenter of a singular
n
-simplex
\sigma
of

X
, and
x^{\prime}
the barycenter of a face of
\sigma
. Since we can always arrange for the

G
-supports of the
B_{\underline{t}}(s)
to be small, the appearance of
g
in the
above formula is not the main problem. The real problem comes from the difference
between
\widehat{f}(x)
and
\widehat{f}(x^{\prime})
. This difference shrinks if we use only
small singular simplices
X
. Therefore, in the construction of a functor

\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(P)\to\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}\times P)
we will need to work with chain
complexes of simplices that get smaller as
{\underline{t}}\to\infty
.

Secondly,

\big((\widehat{f}\times\id_{|P|^{\wedge}})_{*}(\mathbf{S}_{n}(X)\otimes B_{\underline{t}})\big)_{{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}}
typically does
not define an object of
\matheurm{D}^{0}_{G}(\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}}\times P)
. The singular chain complex
is simply to big:
S_{n}(X)
is infinite and so
(7.18d)

will fail. In order to overcome this problem we will replace
X
with a suitable large
ball
D
in
X
(which is compact). We will also replace singular simplices with
simplices from a triangulation of
D
. The set of
n
-simplices
\simp_{n}D
is no
longer a smooth
G
-set, as
D
is not
G
-invariant in
X
, but
\simp_{n}D
will be
invariant for a compact open subgroup of
G
. Moreover, the
G
-action on
X
still
induces a homotopy coherent action of
G
on
D
. Theorem
D.1
provides
maps
X\to|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}(G)|^{\wedge}
. For the restrictions of these maps to large balls
we control the failure of equivariance relative to this homotopy coherent action. Again
we will need the construction to vary in
{\underline{t}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}
.

11.
e
.
Restriction to open subgroups

As discussed in Remark
11.11
, in applications of the
tensor product later on we will not always be able to work with smooth
G
-sets, but will
also need to consider
U
-invariant sets for an open subgroup of
G
.
To formalize this we discuss restrictions and inductions.

Let
U
be an open subgroup of
G
.
Write
\res_{G}^{U}\colon G\text{-}\matheurm{Sets}\to U\text{-}\matheurm{Sets}
for the restriction functor.
It induces a restriction functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Omega),

\displaystyle(\Sigma,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\res_{G}^{U}\Sigma,\res_{G}^{U}c),

that we will also denote by
\res_{G}^{U}
. We write

\ind_{U}^{G}\colon\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
for the canonical inclusion
59
59

59

More precisely,
writing
\res_{G}^{U}\mathcal{B}
for the subcategory of
\mathcal{B}
whose morphisms have support in

U
we have

\ind_{U}^{G}\colon\res_{G}^{U}\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
.
. This inclusion identifies

\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)
with the subcategory of

\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
consisting of all objects and morphisms with

G
-support in
U
. Let us briefly write

\begin{array}[]{rlclcl}\otimes^{G}\colon&\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)&\times&\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)&\to&\Idem\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Omega\times\Lambda);\\
\otimes^{U}\colon&\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Omega)&\times&\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)&\to&\Idem\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}(\Omega\times\Lambda)),\end{array}

for the tensor products. Directly from the definition it follows that for

\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)
we have

(11.12)

\ind_{U}^{G}(\res_{G}^{U}\mathbf{V}\otimes^{U}\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{V}\otimes^{G}\ind_{U}^{G}\mathbf{B}.

Similarly, for morphisms
\rho\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}(\Omega)
and

\varphi\colon\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)
we have

(11.13)

\ind_{U}^{G}(\res_{G}^{U}\rho\otimes^{U}\varphi)=\rho\otimes^{G}\ind_{U}^{G}\varphi.

Because of these identities we will later often drop
\ind_{U}^{G}
and
\res_{G}^{U}
from the
notation and simply write
\otimes=\otimes^{G}=\otimes^{U}
.

11.
f
.
The category

\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)

Let
U
be an open subgroup of
G
. We write
\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)
for
the full subcategory of
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
on all objects with

G
-support in
U
. The induction

\ind_{U}^{G}\colon\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)\to\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
from Subsection
11.e
factors
through the inclusion

\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)
is the full subcategory on objects in the
image of
\ind_{U}^{G}
. Because of support cofinality
(3.2d)
for

\mathcal{B}
the inclusion

\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
induces
an equivalence on idempotent completions. Thus we can often work with

\mathcal{B}_{G,U}(\Lambda)
in place of
\mathcal{B}_{G}(\Lambda)
.

11.
g
.
Tensor product with subcomplexes of

\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)

Assume we are given

(11.14a)

a compact open subgroup
U
of
G
and a compact

U
-invariant subspace
D
of the
U
-fixed points
X^{U}
with a
locally ordered triangulation.

Then we obtain the simplicial chain complex
\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)\in\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}S(X))
, see
Subsection
10.d
. The tensor product with
\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)
then yields a
functor

\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)\,\otimes^{U}\,\--\;\colon\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)\,\to\,\ch\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}(S(X)\times\Lambda)).

Here we do not need the idempotent completion because of Lemma
11.7
.
Write
i\colon\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)\to\res_{G}^{U}\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)
for the inclusion in

\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}S(X))
. Assume we are given in addition

(11.14b)

a chain map
r\colon\res_{G}^{U}\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)\to\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)
in

\ch\mathcal{S}^{U}(\res_{G}^{U}S(X))
with
r\circ i=\id_{\mathbf{C}_{*}(D)}
and a chain homotopy

H\colon i\circ r\simeq\id_{\res_{G}^{U}\mathbf{S}_{*}(X)}
.

In
\ch\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}(S(X)\times\Lambda))
we now obtain for each

\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{U}(\res_{G}^{U}\Lambda)
