14.
e
.
The group
\Gamma

Here we discuss the equivalences (1)
and (2) from (
14.10
).

Fix
P=(G/V_{1},\dots,G/V_{n})
with
V_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}
. Let
K_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
be the
maximal compact open subgroup of
V_{i}
. Set
M:=(G/K_{1},\dots,G/K_{n})
. The quotients

\Gamma_{i}:=V_{i}/K_{i}
are either infinite cyclic or trivial. Let

\Gamma:=\Gamma_{1}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{n}
. Then
\Gamma
is a finitely generated free abelian group of rank at most
n
. There are
canonical maps
h_{i}\colon\Gamma_{i}\to\homendo_{\matheurm{Or}(G)}(G/K_{i})
, sending
cK_{i}\in C_{i}

to
G/K_{i}\to G/K_{i},gK_{i}\mapsto gcK_{i}
. These combine to an action of
\Gamma
on
M

by morphisms in
\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}(G)
. This induces an action of
\Gamma
on

\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)
and we can form
\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)[\Gamma]
. The projection

\pi\colon M\to P
is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the trivial action of
\Gamma
on
P
.
Thus
\pi_{*}\colon\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\to\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P)
is
\Gamma
-equivariant for
the trivial action on
\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P)
and induces a functor

R\colon\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)[\Gamma]\;\to\;\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P).

The functor
R
induces (2) in (
14.10
). We write

\underline{\Gamma}
for the category with exactly one object
\ast_{\Gamma}
whose
endomorphisms are given by
\Gamma
. The action of
\Gamma
on
M
determines a functor

h\colon\underline{\Gamma}\to\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}(G)\downarrow P
that sends
\ast_{\Gamma}
to

\pi\colon M\to P
. In turn
h
induces a map

\hocolimunder_{\underline{\Gamma}}\mathbf{K}\big(\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\big)\;\to\;\hocolimunder_{(Q,f)\in\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}(G)\downarrow P}\mathbf{K}\big(\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(Q)\big);

this is (1) in (
14.10
). The inclusion

\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\to\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)[\Gamma]
induces the assembly map from the
second row of (
14.10
)

(14.11)

\hocolimunder_{\underline{\Gamma}}\mathbf{K}\big(\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\big)\to\mathbf{K}\big(\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)[\Gamma]\big).

Lemma 14.12
.

(a)

The functor
R
induces an equivalence
in K-theory, i.e., (2) in (
14.10
) is an equivalence;

(b)

the functor
h
induces an equivalence,
i.e., (1) in (
14.10
) is an equivalence.

Proof.

(a)

We will first show that for
\mathbf{B}\in\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P)
, there is

(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}},p)\in\Idem\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)
such that
\mathbf{B}\cong R(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}},p)
in

\Idem\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P)
.
Write
\mathbf{B}=(S,[\pi],B)
.
Let
U_{0}\subseteq U_{1}\subset\dots
be a sequence of compact
open subgroups of
G
with
\diam U_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. Write

\pi(s):=(g_{1}(s),\dots,g_{n}(s),t(s))\in G^{n}\times\mathbb{N}=|P|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N}
. Set

K(s):=\supp B(s)\cap g_{1}(s)U_{t(s)}(g_{1}(s))^{-1}\cap\dots\cap g_{n}(s)U_{t(s)}(g_{n}(s))^{-1}.

Using
(3.2d)
we set
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}:=(s,\Pi,B|_{K(s)})
.
By design
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}\in\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(P)
.
Define
i\colon\mathbf{B}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}
and
r\colon\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}\to\mathbf{B}
with

i_{s}^{s^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}i_{B(s),K(s)}&s=s^{\prime}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad r_{s}^{s^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}r_{B(s),K(s)}&s=s^{\prime}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Again by
(3.2d)
we have
r\circ i=\id_{\mathbf{B}}
. Thus

p:=i\circ r
is an idempotent on
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}
and

\mathbf{B}\cong R(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}},p)
as promised. It is an exercise in the definitions to
check that
R
is bijective on morphism sets. Altogether,
R
induces an equivalence on
idempotent completions and thus in K-theory.

(b)
This follows from cofinality
Lemma
A.1
for homotopy colimits; we need to verify that
h
is right
cofinal. Fix
f\colon Q\to P
from
\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}(G)\downarrow P
. We need to show
that
(Q,f)\downarrow h
is contractible. Objects in
(Q,f)\downarrow h
are
morphisms
Q\xrightarrow{g}M
in
\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}(G)
such that
f=\pi\circ g
.
Morphisms in
(Q,f)\downarrow h
are commutative diagrams

\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{P}

We check that
(Q,f)\downarrow h
is equivalent to a point. Recall

P=(G/V_{1},\dots,G/V_{n})
and
M=(G/K_{1},\dots,G/K_{n})
, where
K_{i}
is the maximal
compact open subgroup of
V_{i}
. Write
Q=(G/L_{1},\dots,G/L_{m})
and
f=(u,\varphi)

with
u\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,m\}
and

\varphi(i)\colon G/L_{u(i)}\to G/V_{i}
. Then there are
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in G
with

\varphi(i)yL_{u(i)}=yx_{i}V_{i}
for all
y\in G
, and

x_{i}^{-1}L_{u(i)}x_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
. As
x_{i}^{-1}L_{u(i)}x_{i}
is compact and
K_{i}
is
the unique maximal compact subgroup, we have
x_{i}^{-1}L_{u(i)}x_{i}\subseteq K_{i}
. Let

g:=(u,\psi)\colon Q\to M
with
\psi(i)\colon G/L_{u(i)}\to G/K_{i}
given by

\psi(i)yL_{u(i)}=yx_{i}K_{i}
for all
y\in G
. Then
f=\pi\circ g
. Thus

(Q,f)\downarrow h
is non-empty. If
g^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon Q\to M
is another map
with
f=\pi\circ g^{\prime}
, then
u=u^{\prime}
and there are
v_{i}\in V_{i}
with

\psi^{\prime}(i)yL_{u(i)}=yx_{i}v_{i}K_{i}
. Now the
v_{i}
define an isomorphism

\gamma\colon M\to M
with
\gamma\circ g=g^{\prime}
and
this
\gamma
is the only morphism with this property. Thus any two objects in

(Q,f)\downarrow h
are uniquely isomorphic.
∎

14.
f
.
The category
\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0,\sharp}(M)

Here we discuss the equivalences (3), (4), (5) and (6)
from (
14.10
).

Let
M=(G/K_{1}\times\cdots\times G/K_{n})
be as before. We give a slight variation of
the categories
\matheurm{C}^{+,0}_{G}(M)
and
\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0}_{G}(M)
. The fact that

|M|^{\wedge}\to|M|
has compact fibers (since
M\in P\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}
) will allow us to base
the definition on
|M|
instead of
|M|^{\wedge}
. See also
Remark
5.4
.

Let
\mu_{i}
be a Haar measure on
K_{i}
. We can integrate the left-invariant metric
d_{G}

on
G
to a left-invariant metric
d_{G,i}
on
G
that is in addition right

K
-invariant,
d_{G,i}(g,g^{\prime}):=\int_{K_{i}}d_{G}(gk,g^{\prime}k)d\mu_{i}(k)
and obtain a
left-invariant metric

d_{G/K_{i}}(gK_{i},g^{\prime}K_{i}):=\min_{k\in K}d_{G,i}(gk,g^{\prime})

on
G/K_{i}
.
We obtain a left-invariant metric
d_{|M|}
on
|M|

with

d_{|M|}\big((g_{1}K_{1},\dots,g_{n}K_{n}),(g^{\prime}_{1}K_{1},\dots,g^{\prime}_{n}K_{n})\big):=\max_{i}d_{G/K_{i},}(g_{i}K_{i},g^{\prime}_{i}K_{i}).

We define the
G
-control structure

\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}(M)=\big(\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{1}(M),\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{2}(M),\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)\big)

on
|M|\times\mathbb{N}
as follows:

\bullet

\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{1}(M)
consists of all subsets
F
of
|M|\times\mathbb{N}
for which

F\cap|M|\times\{t\}
is finite for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
;

\bullet

\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{2}(M)
consists of all subsets
E
of

\big(|M|\times\mathbb{N}\big)^{\times 2}
satisfying the following two conditions

–

0
-control over
\mathbb{N}
: if
{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime},t^{\prime}}\\
{\lambda,t}\end{pmatrix}}\in E
then

t=t^{\prime}
;

–

metric control over
|M|
:
for any
\eta>0
there is
t_{0}
such
that for all
t\geq t_{0}
and all
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
we have

{\begin{pmatrix}{\lambda^{\prime},t}\\
{\lambda,t}\end{pmatrix}}\in E\quad\implies\quad d_{|M|}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})<\eta;

\bullet

\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)
consists of all subsets of
G
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}^{\sharp}(M)
be the collection of all subsets
Y
of
|M|\times\mathbb{N}
, for which
there is
d
with
Y\subseteq|M|\times\mathbb{N}_{\leq d}
. We define

\displaystyle\matheurm{C}^{+,0,\sharp}(M)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}(M),\mathcal{Y}^{\sharp}(M));

\displaystyle\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0,\sharp}(M)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{G}(\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}(M),\mathcal{Y}^{\sharp}(M)).

Let
p\colon|M|^{\wedge}\to|M|
be the canonical projection. As the
K_{i}
are compact and
therefore of finite diameter, it is not difficult to check that
\mathfrak{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{2}(M)
is
exactly the image of
\mathfrak{C}^{+,0}_{2}(M)
under

(p\times\id_{\mathbb{N}})^{\times 2}\colon(|M|^{\wedge}\times\mathbb{N})^{\times 2}\to(|M|\times\mathbb{N})^{\times 2}
. This in turn implies that
p\times\id
induces an equivalence

\matheurm{C}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\matheurm{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)
.
Applying (
14.2
)
we obtain the equivalence
\matheurm{C}^{+,0}_{G}(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\matheurm{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)

and this yields the equivalences (3) and (4) from (
14.10
).
Also from (
14.2
) we obtain an equivalence

\matheurm{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{\matheurm{C}}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)
and this induces the
equivalences (5) and (6) from (
14.10
).

14.
g
.
The limit category

We briefly digress to recall a result from
[
4
]
.
Consider a nested sequence of categories

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{C}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{C}_{2}\supseteq\cdots.

Associated to this is the
sequence category

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{*})
. This is the
subcategory of
\prod_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{C}
, whose objects are sequences
(C_{m})_{m\geq 0}

such that for any
l
there is
m_{0}
with
C_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{l}
for all
m\geq m_{0}
.
Morphisms are sequences
(\varphi_{m})_{m\geq 0}
such that for any
l
there is
m_{0}
with

\varphi_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{l}
for all
m\geq m_{0}
. The sum
\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{C}
is a
subcategory of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{*})
and we call the quotient category

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{*}):=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{*})\bigg/\bigoplus_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{0}

the
limit category
. We reviewed
l
-uniform regular coherence and exactness in
Subsection
3.b
. Assume now that

(14.13a)

for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
there is
m_{0}
such that the inclusion

\mathcal{C}_{m+1}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\to\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is exact for all
m\geq m_{0}
;

(14.13b)

for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
there are
m_{0},l\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is
l
-regular coherent for all
m\geq m_{0}
.

Here we use the trivial action to form
\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
.
Then
[
4
, Thm. 14.1]
asserts the following. Consider an
action of a finitely generated free abelian group
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{C}
with the following
property: for
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
there is
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{j}
. Then the assembly map

(14.14)

\hocolimunder_{\underline{\Gamma}}\mathbf{K}\big(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{*})\big)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}\big(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{*})[\Gamma]\big)

is an equivalence. (In
[
4
]
the result is formulated using
an equivariant homology theory instead of a homotopy colimit, but the two formulations are
equivalent, see
[
22
]
.)

14.

.
Analysis of
\matheurm{C}^{+,0,\sharp}_{G}(M)

65
65

65

This ensure that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
defined later carries a

\Gamma

and

-

From this observation it is easy to deduce that there is an equivalence

We point out that for the equivalence

Let now

Moreover

It induces therefore an equivalence

. It follows that (7)

.

Lemma 14.16
.

The nested sequence
\mathcal{C}_{*}
defined
above satisfies
(14.13a)
. If
\mathcal{B}

.

Proof.

We start by examining
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathcal{B}_{G}(\mathfrak{E}^{i})
for

. For

let

be the isotropy group of
\lambda
. This is a
finite intersection of conjugates of the

and thus compact open in
G
. Let

\mathbf{B}=(S,\pi,B)\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
and

. The control

) implies
\supp B(s)\subseteq G_{p_{i}(\pi(s))}
,
in other words
B(s)\in\mathcal{B}|_{G_{p_{i}(\pi(s))}}
. For

\displaystyle F_{i,\lambda}\colon\quad\mathcal{C}_{i}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}|_{G_{\lambda}})_{\oplus}

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{p_{i}(\pi(s))=\lambda}B(s).

By definition of

the set

for all but finitely many

.
The

combine to an equivalence

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{C}_{i}&\xrightarrow{\sim}&\bigoplus_{\lambda\in|Q_{i}|}(\mathcal{B}|_{G_{\lambda}})_{\oplus}\\[3.0pt]
\mathbf{B}&\mapsto&(F_{i,\lambda}(\mathbf{B}))_{\lambda\in|Q_{i}|}.\end{array}

Since the property
l
-uniformly regular passes to direct sums over arbitrary index
sets and is invariant under equivalence of additive categories and the passage going

For

∎

Remark 14.18
.

At least in the case
M\in\matheurm{P}\matheurm{Or}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}(G)
we can give an explanation of the value of

\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{C}_{G}(M)\big)
. Write

M=(G/K_{1},\dots,G/K_{n})
with
K_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{om}}
and fix a
choice of compact open subgroups
(U_{r,i})_{r=1,\dots,n,i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}
of

G
such that for each
r=1,\dots,n
we have

U_{r,1}\supseteq U_{r,2}\supseteq\dots\;\;\supseteq\bigcap_{i}U_{r,i}=K_{r}
.
Put
Q_{i}=(G/K_{1,i},\ldots,G/K_{n,i})
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.

Then there is zigzag of weak homotopy equivalences from
\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{C}_{G}(M)\big)

to the homotopy inverse limit
\holim_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{K}\big(\matheurm{C}_{G}(M_{i})\big)
.
In particular there is for every

n\in\mathbb{Z}
a short exact sequence

0\to\invlim^{1}_{i\to\infty}\pi_{n+1}\bigl(\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{C}_{G}(Q_{i}))\big)\to\pi_{n}\bigl(\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{C}_{G}(M))\big)\\
\to\invlim_{i\to\infty}\pi_{n}\bigl(\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{C}_{G}(Q_{i}))\big)\to 0.

Since we do not need this result in this paper, we omit its proof.
It is very unlikely that the corresponding statement holds, if
we drop the assumption that each
K_{i}
is compact.

Appendix A
Homotopy colimits

A.
i
.
Cofinality and transitivity

We recall two basic facts about homotopy
colimits
[
25
, §9]
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be functor. Let
B\in\mathcal{B}
. We
write
B\downarrow F
for the following category. Objects are pairs

(A,\beta)
with
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
\beta\colon B\to F(A)
in

\mathcal{B}
. Morphisms
(A,\beta)\to(A^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are morphisms

\alpha\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
with

F(\alpha)\circ\beta=\beta^{\prime}
. We write
F\downarrow B
for the
following category. Objects are pairs
(A,\beta)
with
A\in\mathcal{A}

and
\beta\colon F(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{B}
. Morphisms

(A,\beta)\to(A^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are morphisms
\alpha\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in

\mathcal{A}
with
\beta\circ F(\alpha)=\beta^{\prime}
. If
F
is the
inclusion of the subcategory
\mathcal{A}
, then we write

B\downarrow\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\downarrow B
instead of

B\downarrow F
and
F\downarrow B
.

A functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
between small categories is said
to be
right cofinal
, if the nerve of
B\downarrow F
is
contractible (and in particular non-empty) for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma A.1

(Cofinality)
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a right cofinal functor between
small categories. Then for any
\mathbf{D}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
the
canonical map

\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{A}}F^{*}\mathbf{D}\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{D}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

This is
[
25
, 9.4]
.
∎

Lemma A.2

(Transitivity)
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a functor between small
categories. Consider functors
\mathbf{D}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
,

\mathbf{E}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
and a natural transformation

\tau\colon\mathbf{D}\to F^{*}\mathbf{E}
. For
B\in\mathcal{B}
write

v_{B}\colon F\downarrow B\to\mathcal{A}
for the forgetful functor.
Suppose that for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{F\downarrow B}v_{B}^{*}\mathbf{D}\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\mathbf{E}(B)

is an equivalence. Then the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{D}\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{E}

is an equivalence as well.

Proof.

The assumption implies that
\mathbf{E}
is the homotopy push down

F_{*}\mathbf{D}
of
\mathbf{D}
along
F
. The assertion follows
from
[
25
, 9.4]
.
∎

A.
ii
.
Homotopy colimits and categories with product

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a small category with all non-empty finite products.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{+}
be the category obtained from
\mathcal{P}
by adding a
terminal object
\ast
. For example for
\mathcal{P}=\matheurm{P}\mathcal{A}
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{+}=\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt\mathcal{A}
. Let
\mathbf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}_{+}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
be a
functor. The unique maps
P\to\ast
induce the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{D}(P)\;\to\;\mathbf{D}(\ast).

Lemma A.3
.

Fix
Q\in\mathcal{P}
. Then the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{D}(P\times Q)\;\to\;\mathbf{D}(Q)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Consider

\mathcal{P}\;\xrightarrow{f_{Q}}\;\big(\mathcal{P}\downarrow Q\big)\;\xrightarrow{v_{Q}}\;\mathcal{P}\;\xrightarrow{\mathbf{D}}\;\matheurm{Spectra}

where
f_{Q}
sends
P
to the canonical projection
P\times Q\to Q

and
v_{Q}
is the forgetful functor. It is easy to verify that
f_{Q}

is right cofinal. Obviously
(Q,\id_{Q})
is terminal in

\mathcal{P}\downarrow Q
. Thus, using Lemma
A.1
,

\hocolimunder_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{D}(P\times Q)=\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{P}}f_{Q}^{*}v_{Q}^{*}\mathbf{D}\xrightarrow{\sim}\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{P}\downarrow Q}v_{Q}^{*}\mathbf{D}\xrightarrow{\sim}v_{Q}^{*}\mathbf{D}(Q,\id_{Q})=\mathbf{D}(Q).

∎

Proposition A.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small category. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the smallest
collection of functors
\mathbf{E}\colon\mathcal{P}_{+}\mathcal{A}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
that
is closed under hocolimits, retracts and contains all functors of
the form
P\mapsto\mathbf{D}(P\times Q)
with
Q\in\mathcal{P}
and

\mathbf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
. Then for any
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}
the
canonical map

\hocolimunder_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{E}(P)\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\mathbf{E}(\ast).

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Homotopy colimits and retracts of equivalences are again
equivalences. The result follows thus from
Lemma
A.3
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

The nerv of
\mathcal{P}
is contractible (provided that
\mathcal{P}
is
non-empty).

Proof.

Let
Q
be a fixed object of
\mathcal{P}
. Let

c_{Q},x_{Q}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
be the functors
c_{Q}(P)=Q
,
c_{Q}(\varphi)=\id_{Q}
,

x_{Q}(P)=P\times Q
,
x_{Q}(\varphi)=(\varphi\times\id_{Q})
. There are evident natural transformations

\id_{\mathcal{P}}\leftarrow x_{Q}\to c_{Q}
. On the nerv
\id_{\mathcal{P}}
induces
the identity,
c_{Q}
induces a constant map, and the two maps are
homotopic.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a further category with all non-empty finite
products. Let
F\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}
be a product preserving
functor. Assume that
P\downarrow F
is non-empty for all

P\in\mathcal{P}
. Then
F
is right cofinal. In particular, for any

\mathbf{D}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\matheurm{Spectra}
the canonical map

\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{Q}}F^{*}\mathbf{D}\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\hocolimunder_{\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{D}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Using that
\mathcal{Q}
has all non-empty finite products and that
F

preserves finite products, it is not difficult to check that

P\downarrow F
also has all non-empty finite products.
Lemma
A.5
implies that
F

is right cofinal. The statement about homotopy colimits follows
from Lemma
A.1
.
∎

Appendix B
K-theory of
\dg
-categories

In order to obtain induced maps in K-theory for homotopy coherent
functors we use K-theory for
\dg
-categories. We briefly review its
construction. We write
\matheurm{Cat}_{\dg}
for the category of
small
\dg
-categories
[
36
]
. For a
\dg
-category

\mathcal{C}
and objects
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathcal{C}(C,C^{\prime})
for the
chain complex of morphisms from
C
to
C^{\prime}
. For a
\dg
-category

\mathcal{C}
the category
H_{0}(\mathcal{C})
has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and
for
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
the set of morphisms
C\to C^{\prime}
in
H_{0}(\mathcal{C})

is given by
H_{0}(\mathcal{C}(C,C^{\prime}))
. A functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
of
\dg
-categories is a

quasi-equivalence
if

F_{*}\colon\mathcal{C}(C,C^{\prime})\to\mathcal{D}(F(C),F(C^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism for all
C,C^{\prime}
, and it induces an equivalence

H_{0}(\mathcal{C})\to H_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.

Theorem B.1

(
[
19
,
36
,
48
]
)
.

There exists a functor

\mathbf{K}\colon\matheurm{Cat}_{\dg}\to\matheurm{Spectra}

with the following properties:

(a)

Restricted to additive
categories
\mathbf{K}
is weakly equivalent to the usual
(non-connective) K-theory
functor;

(b)

For an additive
category
\mathcal{A}
the inclusion of
\mathcal{A}
into the category

\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}\mathcal{A}
of finite chain complexes induces a weak
equivalence
\mathbf{K}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbf{K}(\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A}))
;

(c)

If

F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\matheurm{Cat}_{\dg}
is a
quasi-equivalence, then
\mathbf{K}(F)
is a weak equivalence.

Sketch of proof.

K-theory of a
\dg
-category
\mathcal{C}
can be defined as the
Waldhausen K-theory of the category of perfect

\mathcal{C}
-modules
[
36
, Sec. 5.2]

and
[
19
]
.

We review the construction of
Schlichting
[
48
, Sec. 6.4]
66
66

66

Strictly
speaking Schlichting only considers
\operatorname{dga}
s, not

\dg
-categories, but his construction generalizes in a straight
forward manner.
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\dg
-category. A

\mathcal{C}
-module is a
\dg
-functor

M\colon\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to\ch(\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod})
. A map of

\mathcal{C}
-modules
M\to M^{\prime}
is a natural transformation by chain
maps. A
\mathcal{C}
-module is free on
v\in M(C)_{k}
if

f\mapsto f^{*}(v)
defines an isomorphism
\mathcal{C}(-,C)[k]\to M(-)

of
\mathcal{C}
-modules. A finite cell
\mathcal{C}
-module is a

\mathcal{C}
-module
M
together with a finite filtration

M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq M_{n}=M
such that the
quotients
M_{i}/M_{i-1}
are free on a finite number of
generators. We write
\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
for the
category of finite cell
\mathcal{C}
-modules. If
\mathcal{A}
is an additive
category, then the category of finite cell
\mathcal{A}
-modules can be
identified with the
\dg
-category
\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
of finite
chain complexes over
\mathcal{A}
. For
\mathcal{C}=\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
the
Yoneda embedding of
\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
into the category of

\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
-modules identifies
\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
with

\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
-
\matheurm{cell}
. A sequence

M\xrightarrow{i}M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p}M^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
is said to be
exact
, if

M(C)\to M^{\prime}(C)\to M^{\prime\prime}(C)
is exact in
\ch(\mathbb{Z})
for each

C\in\mathcal{C}
. With this notion of exact sequences

\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
is an exact category.

The exact category
\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
has enough injectives
and projectives.
67
67

67

I
is injective (projective) in an exact
category
\mathcal{E}
if for any exact sequence

A\xrightarrow{i}B\xrightarrow{p}C
the map

\mathcal{E}(B,I)\xrightarrow{i^{*}}\mathcal{E}(A,I)
(the map

\mathcal{E}(P,B)\xrightarrow{p_{*}}\mathcal{E}(P,C)
) is
onto
[
35
, Sec. 5]
.
The classes of
projectives and injectives in this exact category coincide and are
given by the contractible cell modules. A map

\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
factors over an injective (projective), if
and only if it chain nullhomotopic. (All this can be proven using
cones of
\mathcal{C}
-modules exactly as
in
[
48
, Sec.6.4]
.)

A
Frobenius category
is an exact category
\mathcal{E}
with enough
projectives and injectives such that the classes of injectives
and projectives coincide. The associated stable category is the
quotient of
\mathcal{E}
by the subcategory of projectives (injectives),
i.e., morphisms are identified if they factor over a projective
(injective). A map of Frobenius categories is an exact functor that
preserves projectives (injectives). The category of cell modules

\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
is a Frobenius category and the associated
stable category is the homotopy category of

\mathcal{C}
-
\matheurm{cell}
.

Schlichting’s
[
48
, Sec. 12.1]
non-connective
K-theory functor

\mathbf{K}^{\text{Frob}}\colon\text{Frobenius categories}\to\matheurm{Spectra}

has the following property: if a map
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of Frobenius
categories induces an equivalence of stable categories, then

\mathbf{K}^{\text{Frob}}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbf{K}^{\text{Frob}}(\mathcal{E})
is an
equivalence
[
48
, Cor. 1]
.

For a
\dg
-category
\mathcal{C}
one then defines

\mathbf{K}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathbf{K}^{\text{Frob}}(\mathcal{C}\text{-}\matheurm{cell}).

For an additive category
\mathcal{A}
we have

\mathcal{A}
-
\matheurm{cell}\simeq\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}(\mathcal{A})
, see above.
Now
(a)
follows
from
[
48
, Thm. 5]
.
For
(b)
we can use that for an additive
category
\mathcal{A}
the inclusion
\mathcal{A}\to\ch_{fin}(\mathcal{A})
induces
an equivalence on cell modules and therefore in K-theory.
For
(c)
we use that for a
quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
the induced functor on the
stable categories associated to the categories of cell modules
(i.e., on the homotopy categories of cell modules) is an
equivalence.
∎

We record the following consequence of the quasi-isomorphism
invariance of K-theory.

Corollary B.2
.

Let

F,F^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be
\dg
-functors and let

\tau\colon F\to F^{\prime}
be a natural transformation of

\dg
-functors. Assume that
H_{0}(\tau)\colon H_{0}(F)\to H_{0}(F^{\prime})

is an isomorphism between the functors

H_{0}(F),H_{0}(F^{\prime})\colon H_{0}(\mathcal{C})\to H_{0}(\mathcal{D})
. Then
\mathbf{K}(F)

and
\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime})
are homotopic.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem
B.1

(c)

using the path
\dg
-category
P(\mathcal{D})
associated to
\mathcal{D}

(see
[
24
, 2.9]

and
[
50
, Def. 4.1]
) by a standard argument.

Objects of
P(\mathcal{D})
are diagrams
D\xrightarrow{f}D^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}

with
f
closed of degree
0
whose homology class is an isomorphism
in
H_{0}(\mathcal{D})
. Given objects
D_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}D^{\prime}_{0}
,

D_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}D^{\prime}_{1}
, the complex

P(\mathcal{D})(D_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}D^{\prime}_{0},D_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}D^{\prime}_{1})
is
the homotopy fiber of

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(D_{0},D^{\prime}_{0})\oplus\mathcal{D}(D_{1},D^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(D_{0},D^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle f_{1}\circ\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\circ f_{0}.

Composition is defined by the formula

(\varphi_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1},s_{1})\circ(\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\prime}_{0},s_{0}):=(\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\circ\varphi^{\prime}_{0},\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\circ s_{0}+s_{1}\circ\varphi_{0}).

There are
\dg
-functors
p,p^{\prime}\colon P(\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{D}
with

p(D\xrightarrow{f}D^{\prime})=D
,
p^{\prime}(D\xrightarrow{f}D^{\prime})=D^{\prime}
. We
also have
i\colon\mathcal{D}\to P(\mathcal{D})
with

i(D)=(D\xrightarrow{\id_{D}}D)
and

\Psi\colon\mathcal{C}\to P(\mathcal{D})
with

\Psi(C)=(F(C)\xrightarrow{\tau_{C}}F^{\prime}(C))
. Then

p\circ i=p^{\prime}\circ i=\id_{\mathcal{D}}
. Moreover,
i
is a
quasi-isomorphism
[
50
, Lem. 4.3]
. Using
Theorem
B.1

(c)

this implies
\mathbf{K}(p)\simeq\mathbf{K}(p^{\prime})
. We also have

F=p\circ\Psi
and
F^{\prime}=p^{\prime}\circ\Psi
. Thus

\mathbf{K}(F)\simeq\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime})
.
∎

Appendix C
Homotopy coherent functors

Our construction of the transfer depends on induced maps in K-theory
for homotopy coherent functors. Here we give a self contained
construction of such induced maps that is tailored to our specific
needs. It is very pedestrian, by no means a complete theory, and
makes no direct contact with the elegant language of stable

\infty
-categories. We include this for completeness.

Definition C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be
additive categories. A
homotopy coherent functor

F=(F^{0},F^{1},\dots)\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
consists of the
following data.

(C.1a)

For any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
an object
F^{0}(A)\in\ch\mathcal{B}
;

(C.1b)

For any chain

A_{0}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{1}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{2}}\dots\xleftarrow{\varphi_{n}}A_{n}

of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
a map

F^{n}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\colon F^{0}(A_{n})\to F^{0}(A_{0})
of
degree
n-1
.

F
is required to satisfy

dF^{n}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{j}\Big(F^{n-1}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{j}\circ\varphi_{j+1},\dots\varphi_{n})\\
-F^{j}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{j})\circ F^{n-j}(\varphi_{j+1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\Big).

Here
d
is the differential in

\hom_{\ch\mathcal{B}}(F(A_{n}),F(A_{0}))
68
68

68

We use

d(\psi)=d_{B^{\prime}}\circ\psi-(-1)^{|\psi|}\psi\circ d_{B}
for

\psi\colon B\to B^{\prime}
in
\ch\mathcal{B}
of degree
|\psi|
.
. The

F^{n}
are also be required to be multi-linear in the
\varphi_{i}
.

Example C.2
.

Every functor

f\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
can be viewed as a homotopy coherent
functor
F
with
F^{0}(A)=f(A)
,
F^{1}(\varphi)=f(\varphi)
,

F^{n}\equiv 0
for
n\geq 2
.

Example C.3

(Deformation of a functor to a homotopy coherent functor)
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear functor.
Suppose we are given for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
a diagram in
\ch\mathcal{B}

B_{A}\xrightarrow{i_{A}}f(A)\xrightarrow{r_{A}}B_{A}

with
r_{A}\circ i_{A}=\id_{B_{A}}
and a chain homotopy

H_{A}\colon i_{A}\circ r_{A}\simeq\id_{f(A)}
. Then we obtain a
homotopy coherent functor
F=(F^{0},F^{1},\dots)
with
F(A)=B_{A}

for
A\in\mathcal{A}
and

F^{n}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})=\\
r_{A_{0}}\circ f(\varphi_{1})\circ H_{A_{1}}\circ f(\varphi_{2})\circ\dots\circ f(\varphi_{n-1})\circ H_{A_{n-1}}\circ f(\varphi_{n})\circ i_{A_{n}}\hskip 12.91663pt

for

A_{0}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{1}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{2}}\dots\xleftarrow{\varphi_{n}}A_{n}

in
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition C.4
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
be
homotopy coherent functors. A strict natural transformation

\tau\colon F\to G
consists of chain maps

\tau_{A}\colon F^{0}(A)\to G^{0}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
such that
for any chain

A_{0}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{1}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{2}}\dots\xleftarrow{\varphi_{n}}A_{n}

of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
the diagram

\textstyle{F(A_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})}
\textstyle{G(A_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G^{n}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})}
\textstyle{F(A_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{A_{0}}}
\textstyle{G(A_{0})}

commutes.

Example C.5
.

Let

f\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
,

B_{A}\xrightarrow{i_{A}}f(A)\xrightarrow{r_{A}}B_{A}

and
H_{A}
be as in
Example
C.3
. Let

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
be the homotopy coherent functor
associated to this data.

Let
g\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
a further functor, which we also
view as a homotopy coherent functor, see
Example
C.2
.

Let
\tau\colon f\to g
be a natural transformation. Suppose we
are given chain maps
p_{A}\colon B_{A}\to g(A)
such that

\tau_{A}=p_{A}\circ r_{A}
,
p_{A}=\tau_{A}\circ i_{A}
,

0=\tau_{A}\circ H_{A}
. Then
p_{A}
determines a strict natural
transformation
F\to g
.

Definition C.6
.

We write
\matheurm{Int}
for the following category. The objects of
\matheurm{Int}

are linearly ordered sets of the form

[0,n]:=\{0<1<\cdots<n\}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Maps

[0,n]\to[0,m]
are strictly order preserving maps

\sigma\colon\{0<\cdots<n\}\to\{0<\cdots<m\}
with

\sigma(0)=0
,
\sigma(n)=\sigma(m)
. In particular
\sigma
is
injective and
n\leq m
.

Remark C.7
.

There is an identification
\Delta_{\text{inj}}^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\matheurm{Int}
. Here
\Delta
is the usual simplicial category of finite
ordered sets of the form
\{0<\cdots<n\}
and

\Delta_{\text{inj}}
is the subcategory obtained by restricting to
injective maps.

Remark C.8
.

Often we will identify
[0,n]\in\matheurm{Int}
with
the category associated to
[0,n]
as a poset. Note that a
composable chain of morphisms

A_{0}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{1}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{2}}\dots\xleftarrow{\varphi_{n}}A_{n}

in
\mathcal{A}
is the same thing as a functor
[0,n]^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Moreover, any finite linearly order set
I
with at least two
elements is canonically isomorphic to some
[0,n]
. In particular,
we can evaluate any homotopy coherent functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
on any functor
I^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Definition C.9
.

For
[0,n]\in\ob\matheurm{Int}
we define the chain complex
C_{*}([0,n])
as
follows. As an abelian group
C_{*}([0,n])
is generated by pairs

(I,J)
with
\{0<n\}\subseteq J\subseteq I\subseteq[0,n]
.
The degree of
(I,J)
is the cardinality
|I\setminus J|
of

I\setminus J
. The boundary
d
is determined as follows. Write

I\setminus J=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\}
.
Then

(I,J)\mapsto\sum_{j=0}^{l}(-1)^{j}\Big((I\setminus\{i_{j}\},J)-(I,J\cup\{i_{j}\})\Big).

For
\sigma\colon[0,n]\to[0,m]
we define

\sigma_{*}\colon C_{*}([0,n])\to C_{*}([0,m])
by

\sigma_{*}(I,J):=\begin{cases}(\sigma(I),\sigma(J))&|I\setminus J|=|\sigma(I)\setminus\sigma(J)|\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Altogether we obtain the functor
69
69

69

In the language
of
[
22
, end of Section 3]
this is the
covariant
\mathbb{Z}\matheurm{Int}
-chain complex

C_{*}(E^{\operatorname{bar}}\matheurm{Int})
for
E^{\operatorname{bar}}\matheurm{Int}

the bar-model for the covariant classifying
\matheurm{Int}
-
CW
-complex of
the category
\matheurm{Int}
.

C_{*}\colon\matheurm{Int}\to\ch(\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod}).

Remark C.10
.

The chain complex
C_{*}([0,n])
can also be described as the cellular
chain complex of the
(n\,\text{-})1
-dimensional cube.

Remark C.11
.

In the category of functors
\matheurm{Int}\to\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod}
the complex

C_{*}(-)
is degreewise free; we have

C_{k}(-)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\{0<i\}\subseteq J\subseteq[0,i],\\
|[0,i]\setminus J|=k\end{array}}\mathbb{Z}\matheurm{Int}(J,-).

Remark C.12
.

There is a concatenation map

(C.13)

C_{*}([0,n])\otimes C_{*}([0,m])\to C_{*}([0,n+m])

defined by

(I,J)\otimes(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})\mapsto(I\cup n+I^{\prime},J\cup n+J^{\prime})
.

Lemma C.14
.

The augmentation map

\epsilon\colon C_{*}([0,n])\to\mathbb{Z}
with
\epsilon(I,I)=1
is a
homology isomorphism.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. For
n=1
the augmentation is an
isomorphism. For
n>1
we define
\tilde{C}_{*}([0,n])
as the
sub-complex of
C_{*}([0,n])
spanned by all
(I,J)
with

1\notin I
. Clearly
\tilde{C}_{*}([0,n])\cong C_{*}([0,{n-1}])
.
The map defined by

(I,J)\mapsto\begin{cases}(I,J\cup\{1\})&1\in I\setminus J\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

induces a contraction on the quotient

C_{*}([0,n])/\tilde{C}_{*}([0,n])
. Thus the inclusion

\tilde{C}_{*}([0,n])\to C_{*}([0,{n}])
induces an isomorphism in
homology.
∎

In the following we abbreviate
\mathcal{A}(A,A^{\prime}):=\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})
.

Definition C.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small
\mathbb{Z}
-linear category. For
[0,n]\in\matheurm{Int}

and
A,A^{\prime}\in\ob\mathcal{A}
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime}):=\bigoplus_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n-1}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(A_{1},A^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle(A_{2},A_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{A}(A,A_{n-1}).

There is an evident concatenation map

(C.16)

\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{[0,m]}(A,A^{\prime})\to\mathcal{A}_{[0,n+m]}(A,A^{\prime\prime}).

Using composition in
\mathcal{A}
, this construction is functorial in

\matheurm{Int}
and we obtain for fixed
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
a contravariant
functor

\mathcal{A}_{\--}(A,A^{\prime})\colon\matheurm{Int}\to\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod};\quad[0,n]\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime}).

Definition C.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small
\mathbb{Z}
-linear category. The
\dg
-category

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
is defined as follows. The objects of

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
are the objects of
\mathcal{A}
. For objects

A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
the chain complex of morphisms is defined as

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime}):=\mathcal{A}_{\--}(A,A^{\prime})\otimes_{\matheurm{Int}}C_{*}(\--).

Here
\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime})
is viewed as a chain complex concentrated
in degree
0
. Composition is induced from the concatenation
maps (
C.13
) and (
C.16
).

Construction C.18
.

We construct a functor
\comp\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}

as follows. On objects the functor is the identity. Let

A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
. Then

\comp\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\to\mathcal{A}(A,A^{\prime})

is defined as follows. For

f\in\big(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\big)_{>0}
we set

\comp(\varphi)=0
. Let

f\in\big(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\big)_{0}
. We can write

f=(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\otimes(I,I)
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\{0,1\}\subseteq I\subseteq[0,n]
. Then

\comp(f)=\varphi_{1}\circ\dots\circ\varphi_{n}
.

Lemma C.19
.

The functor

\comp\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}
is a
quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

The objects of
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{A}
coincide. It remains to
show that for all
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}

\comp\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\to\mathcal{A}(A,A^{\prime})

is a chain homotopy equivalence. Here
\mathcal{A}(A,A^{\prime})
is viewed as
chain complex concentrated in degree
0
.

By Lemma
C.14
the augmentation

\epsilon\colon C_{*}([0,n])\to\mathbb{Z}
is a homology isomorphism. As

\matheurm{Int}([0,1],[0,n])
consists of a single morphism we obtain a
homology isomorphism

C_{*}(-)\to\mathbb{Z}\big[\matheurm{Int}([0,1],-)\big],

of chain complexes in the category of functors

\matheurm{Int}\to\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\matheurm{Mod}
. As both are free, see
Remark
C.11
, it is a chain homotopy equivalence.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime})\otimes_{[0,n]\in\matheurm{Int}}C_{*}([0,n])

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime})\otimes_{[0,n]\in\matheurm{Int}}\mathbb{Z}\big[\matheurm{Int}([0,1],[0,n])\big]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{[0,1]}(A,A^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}(A,A^{\prime}).

∎

Construction C.20
.

Let

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
be a homotopy coherent functor. We
define a functor

F_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
as follows.
On objects the functor is given by
A\mapsto F(A)
. For

A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\to{\ch\mathcal{B}}(F(A),F(A^{\prime}))

is induced from the maps

(C.21)

\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime})\otimes C_{*}([0,n])\to{\ch\mathcal{B}}\big(F(A),F(A^{\prime})\big)

that we define next. Let

\varphi_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\varphi_{n}\otimes(I,J)\in\mathcal{A}_{[0,n]}(A,A^{\prime})\otimes C_{*}([0,n])
. We obtain a functor

\phi\colon[0,n]^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{A}
that sends the map
l\to l+1
in

[0,n]
to
f_{l+1}
. Write

J=\{j_{0}=0<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}=n\}
and set

J_{r-1,r}:=\{j_{r-1}<j_{r-1}+1<\cdots<j_{r}\}\cap I
for

r=1,\ldots,k
. Recall that we can evaluate
F
on any
(contravariant) functor from a finite linear ordered set to
\mathcal{A}
.
Now (
C.21
) sends

\varphi_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\varphi_{n}\otimes(I,J)
to

F(\phi|_{J_{k-1,k}})\circ F(\phi|_{J_{k-2,k-1}})\circ\dots\circ F(\phi|_{J_{1,2}})\circ F(\phi|_{J_{0,1}}).

Verifying that this yields a well-defined functor
F_{\mathfrak{C}}

is a lengthy but not difficult computation, that we omit.

Remark C.22
.

The construction of
\comp\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}
is the
special case of
Construction
C.20
applied to

\id_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Remark C.23
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch\mathcal{B}
be homotopy coherent functors
and
\tau\colon F\to G
be a strict natural transformation. Then

\tau
also defines a natural transformation

F_{\mathfrak{C}}\to G_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Remark C.24
.

Let

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}\mathcal{B}
be a homotopy coherent
functor. We can apply K-theory to

\mathcal{A}\xleftarrow{\comp}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{F_{\mathfrak{C}}}\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}\mathcal{B}\xleftarrow{i}\mathcal{B}

where
i
is the inclusion. Using Theorem
B.1

we obtain an induced zig-zag in K-theory

(C.25)

\mathbf{K}\mathcal{A}\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{K}(F_{\mathfrak{C}})}\mathbf{K}\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}\mathcal{B}\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}\mathcal{B}.

Remark C.26
.

Let

F,G\colon\mathcal{A}\to\ch_{{\mathrm{fin}}}\mathcal{B}
be homotopy coherent
functors and
\tau\colon F\to G
be a strict natural
transformation. Then
\tau
also defines a natural transformation

F_{\mathfrak{C}}\to G_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Suppose that

\tau_{A}\colon F^{0}(A)\to G^{0}(A)
is a weak equivalence for all

A\in\mathcal{A}
.
Corollary
B.2

implies that then

\mathbf{K}(F_{\mathfrak{C}})\simeq\mathbf{K}(G_{\mathfrak{C}})
. In other
words the zig-zags (
C.25
) induced in
K-theory by
F
and
G
agree up to homotopy.

Appendix D
Mapping
X
to
|\mathbf{J}_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}}(G)|^{\wedge}

Let
G
be a reductive
p
-adic group and let
X
be the associated
extended Bruhat-Tits building. The extended Bruhat-Tits building
X
can be given a
simplicial structure for which the action of
G
is simplicial, proper
and smooth. We will also use the existence of a
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
-metric

d_{X}
on
X
that generates the topology of
X
and is
G
-invariant.
We fix a base point
x_{0}\in X
. We write
B_{R}
for the closed ball
of radius
R
centered at
x_{0}
and
\pi_{R}\colon X\to B_{R}
for the
radial projection.

For a collection of subgroups
\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}
we will
consider the
\matheurm{P}\hskip-2.5pt_{+}\hskip-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.3pt\mathrm{ll}}(G)
-simplicial complex
\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}(G)
from
Example
6.4
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
we will also use

\mathbf{J}^{N}_{\mathcal{V}}(G)
from Example
6.4
. Recall that the
simplicial complex
J^{N}_{\mathcal{V}}(G)
underlying
\mathbf{J}^{N}_{\mathcal{V}}(G)
is a
finite complex if
\mathcal{V}
is finite. We will also need the foliated
distance
d_{\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
on
|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)|^{\wedge}

(defined in Subsection
7.c
). Recall
that it is compatible with restrictions to subcomplexes, i.e.,

d_{\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}=d_{\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}^{N^{\prime}}(G)\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}|_{|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)|^{\wedge}}
for

N\leq N^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. To ease notation we abbreviate

d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}:=d_{\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
.

The following result is
[
6
, Thm. 1.2]

for td-groups with an
action on
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
-space satisfying a technical assumption. This assumption is satisfied
for the action of a reductive
p
-adic group on its extended Bruhat-Tits
building
[
6
, Prop. A.7]
.

Theorem D.1

(
X
to
J
)
.

There is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
M\subseteq G
compact and

\epsilon>0
there are
\beta>0
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}

finite with the following property. For all
\eta>0
and all

L>0
we find
R>0
and a (not necessarily continuous) map

f\colon X\to|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)|^{\wedge}
satisfying:

(a)

for
x\in B_{R+L}
,
g\in M
we
have
d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(f(gx),gf(x))<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)
;

(b)

for
x\in B_{R+L}
,
R^{\prime}\geq R
we
have
d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(f(x),f(\pi_{R^{\prime}}(x)))<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)
;

(c)

there is
\rho>0
such that for
all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
with
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})<\rho
we have

d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)
.

The three assertions appearing in Theorem
D.1
correspond
to
(12.2a)
,
(12.2b)
and
(12.2c)
from
Assumption
12.2
.

The goal in this section is to outline the proof Theorem
D.1
.
A detailed construction is
given in
[
6
, Thm. 1.2]
.

D.
i
.
The flow space

We briefly recall the flow space
\mathit{FS}
associated to
X

from
[
3
, Sec. 1]
. It consists of all

generalized geodesic
, i.e., of continuous maps

c\colon\mathbb{R}\to X
whose restriction to some close
interval
70
70

70

By this we mean a subset of the form
(-\infty,b]
,

[a,\infty)
,
(-\infty,\infty)
, or
[a,b]
.
is an isometric
embedding and is locally constant on the complement of this interval.
The metric on
\mathit{FS}
is given by

d_{\mathit{FS}}(c,c^{\prime}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d_{X}(c(t),c^{\prime}(t))}{2e^{|t|}}\,dt.

In this metric the distance between
c
and
c^{\prime}
is small, iff the
restrictions
c|_{[-a,a]}
and
c^{\prime}|_{[-a,a]}
are pointwise close for
large
a
. We recall
from
[
3
, Prop. 1.7]
that this metric
generates the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets. The
flow
\Phi
on
\mathit{FS}
is defined by

(\Phi_{\tau}c)(t):=c(t+\tau).

For
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathit{FS}
,
\alpha,\delta>0
we write

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)

if there is
t\in[-\alpha,\alpha]
with

d_{\mathit{FS}}(\Phi_{t}(c),c^{\prime})<\delta
.

The construction is natural for the action of the isometry group of

X
. In particular,
G
acts on
\mathit{FS}
, the flow
\Phi_{\tau}
is

G
-equivariant, and
d_{\mathit{FS}}
and
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
are both
G
-invariant.

D.
ii
.
V
-foliated distance and the flow space

There is a close relation between the foliated distance
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}

on the flow space and the
V
-foliated distance from
Subsection
5.c
. We discuss the case

G=\SL_{2}(F)
. We will not explicitly use elsewhere what follows,
but this was our motivation for the definition of
V
-foliated
distance.

The building
X
for
\SL_{2}(F)
is a simplicial tree, the Bass-Serre
tree. We normalize the metric on
X
such that each edge has length

1
. Let us say that a
bi-infinite combinatorial geodesic
is
an isometric embedding
c\colon\mathbb{R}\to X
that sends

\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
into the
0
-skeleton
X^{0}
of the Bass-Serre
tree
71
71

71

Bi-infinite combinatorial geodesics are uniquely
determined by their restriction to
\mathbb{Z}
and any isometric embedding

\mathbb{Z}\to X^{0}
extends uniquely to a bi-infinite combinatorial
geodesic.
. The bi-infinite combinatorial geodesics form a closed
subspace
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
of
\mathit{FS}
. The flow on
\mathit{FS}
restricts to an

\mathbb{Z}
action on
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
. Fix
c\in\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
and let
K_{c}
be
the (pointwise) stabilizer of
c
and
V_{c}
be the stabilizer of the
image of
c
in the quotient of
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
by the
\mathbb{Z}
-action,
compare
D.vi
. There is a choice of
c
such
that
K_{c}
is the subgroup of diagonal matrices in
\SL_{2}(\mathcal{O})
,
where
\mathcal{O}
is the ring of integers of
F
and
V_{c}
is the subgroup
of diagonal matrices in
\SL_{2}(F)
, but this will not be important in
the following. Define
\pi\colon G\to\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
as

g\mapsto gc
. As the action of
\SL_{2}(F)
on
X
is strongly
transitive, the induced action on
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
is transitive. Thus we
can identify
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
with
G/K_{c}
via
\pi
. The group

V_{c}/K_{c}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}\cong c(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq X^{0}
by translation.
This induces a group homomorphism
t\colon V_{c}\to\mathbb{Z}
, that in turn
induces an isomorphism
\bar{t}\colon V_{c}/K_{c}\cong\mathbb{Z}
. The
quotient
V_{c}/K_{c}
acts from the right on
G/K_{c}
and under

G/K_{c}\equiv\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
and
V_{c}/K_{c}\cong\mathbb{Z}
this action is the

\mathbb{Z}
-action on
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
induced from the flow on
\mathit{FS}
. Recall
that
d_{V_{c}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
on
G
depended on the choice of a left invariant
proper metric
d_{G}
on
G
. The metric
d_{\mathit{FS}}
restricts to a metric
on
\mathit{FS}^{\sharp}
. As
\pi
is
G
-equivariant it is in particular
uniformly continuous. As
d_{G}|_{V_{c}}
is proper and
V_{c}
-invariant
and as
K_{c}
is compact,
t\colon V_{c}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a coarse
equivalence. This means that for all
A>0
there is
B>0
such
that for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{c}

\begin{array}[]{rcr}d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})\leq A&\implies&|t(v)-t(v^{\prime})|\leq B;\\
|t(v)-t(v^{\prime})|\leq A&\implies&d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})\leq B.\end{array}

Combining all this we have the following: for all
\alpha>0
there
is
\beta>0
, such that for
\epsilon>0
there is
\delta>0

such that for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
,

\begin{array}[]{rcr}d_{V_{c}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(g,g^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)&\implies&d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(\pi(g),\pi(g^{\prime}))<(\beta,\epsilon);\\
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(\pi(g),\pi(g^{\prime}))<(\alpha,\delta)&\implies&d_{V_{c}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(g,g^{\prime})<(\beta,\epsilon).\end{array}

Thus we can translate between
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
and
d_{V_{c}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
.

D.
iii
.
Factorization over the flow space

The maps in Theorem
D.1
are constructed in two steps as
compositions

X\;\xrightarrow{f_{0}}\;\mathit{FS}\;\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\;|\mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(G)|^{\wedge};

Theorem D.2

(
X
to
\mathit{FS}
)
.

For all
M\subseteq G
compact there is
\alpha>0
with the
following property. For all
\delta>0
,
L>0
there exists

R>0
and a uniformly continuous map
f_{0}\colon X\to\mathit{FS}
such
that

(a)

for
x\in B_{R+L}
,
g\in M
we have

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}\big(f_{0}(gx),gf_{0}(x)\big)<(\alpha,\delta)
;

(b)

for
x\in{B}_{R+L}
,
R^{\prime}\geq R
we
have

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}\big(f_{0}(x),f_{0}(\pi_{R^{\prime}}(x))\big)<(\alpha,\delta)
,
where
\pi_{R^{\prime}}
denotes the radial projection onto

\overline{B}_{R+L}
.

Theorem D.3

(
\mathit{FS}
to
J
)
.

There is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
\alpha>0
and any

\epsilon>0
there are
\beta>0
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}

finite such that for any
\eta>0
there are
\delta>0
,

f_{1}\colon\mathit{FS}\to|\mathbf{J}^{N}_{\mathcal{V}}|^{\wedge}
, satisfying the following
properties.

(a)

For

c,c^{\prime}\in\mathit{FS}
with
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)
we have

d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}\big(f_{1}(c),f_{1}(c^{\prime})\big)<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)
;

(b)

For
c\in\mathit{FS}
,

g\in G
we have

d_{\mathbf{J}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}\big(f_{1}(gc),gf_{1}(c)\big)<(\beta,\eta,\epsilon)
.

Theorem
D.2
is
[
6
, Thm. 4.1]
.
Its proof uses the
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
-geometry of

X
and associated dynamic properties of the flow space and is
sketched in Subsection
D.iv
.
Theorem
D.3
is
[
6
, Thm. 4.3]
.
Its proof uses so called long and thin covers for the
flow space and is sketched in
Subsection
D.v
.

Theorem
D.1
is a formal consequence of
Theorems
D.2
and
D.3
. Formally this uses
that for any
\alpha>0
,
\delta>0
there is
\rho>0
such that

d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})<\rho
implies

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(f_{1}(x),f_{1}(x^{\prime}))<(\alpha,\delta)
. This statement
follows from uniform continuity of
f_{1}
(even uniformly in

\alpha
).

D.
iv
.
Dynamic of the flow

Theorem
D.2
is closely related to the results
from
[
3
, Sec. 3]

and follows from
similar estimates
72
72

72

The present set-up is simpler and avoids
the homotopy action in our estimates.
. We sketch its proof here.

For
x,x^{\prime}\in X
we write
c_{x,x^{\prime}}\in\mathit{FS}
for the generalized
geodesic from
x
to
x^{\prime}
, i.e., for the generalized geodesic
characterized by

\displaystyle c_{x,x^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\quad t\in(-\infty,0];

\displaystyle c_{x,x^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\quad t\in[d(x,y),+\infty).

For
T\geq 0
consider the map
f_{1}^{T}\colon X\to\mathit{FS}
,

x\mapsto\Phi_{T}(c_{x_{0},x})
. Recall that
x_{0}\in X
is our fixed
base point. Both
x\mapsto c_{x_{0},x}
and
\Phi_{T}
(for fixed
T
)
are uniformly continuous, in particular,
f_{1}^{T}
is uniformly
continuous.

Lemma D.4
.

For all
\delta>0
there is
\Delta>0
such that for all
R^{\prime},T

with
R^{\prime}\geq T+\Delta
,
x\in X
we have

d_{\mathit{FS}}\big(f_{1}^{T}(x),f_{1}^{T}(\pi_{R^{\prime}}(x))\big)<\delta.

x_{0}
\Phi_{T}(c_{x_{0},x})(0)=\Phi_{T}(c_{x_{0},\pi_{R^{\prime}}(x)}(0)
\pi_{R^{\prime}}(x)
x
=T
\geq\Delta

Sketch of proof for Lemma

.

For large
\Delta
the generalized geodesics

and

agree on a large
interval
[-a,a]
, see Figure

, and are
therefore close to each other in
\mathit{FS}
.
For more details see

.
∎

Lemma D.5
.

For all
\alpha>0
,

,
L>0
, and

\delta>0
there are
R>0
,
0\leq T\leq R-\Delta
such that
for all

with

, we have

gx_{0}
x_{0}
gx
\Phi_{T}(gc_{x_{0},x})(0)

\Phi_{T+\tau}(c_{x_{0},gx})(0)

and

. Let

. By assumption

. Now for

and

,

Sketch of proof for Theorem
D.2
.

We set
\alpha:=\max\{d_{X}(gx_{0},x_{0})\mid g\in M\}
. Given

\delta>0
,
L>0
we first choose
\Delta
as in
Lemma
D.4
and use then

to choose
R
and

. Then

f_{1}^{T}\colon X\to\mathit{FS}
has the desired properties:
for

and for

.

D.
v
.
Long thin covers

Definition D.6

(
\alpha
-long cover)
.

A cover
\mathcal{U}
of the flow space
\mathit{FS}
by open subsets is said to
be
\alpha
-long
if for any
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}
there exists

U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\Phi_{[-\alpha,\alpha]}(c_{0})\subseteq U
.

Typically such covers are thin in directions transversal to the flow
and are often referred to as long thin covers. The proof of
Theorem
D.3
uses the following three results for the
flow space.

Proposition D.7

(Partition of unity)
.

For all
\alpha>0
,

{\varepsilon}>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
there is
\alpha^{\prime}>0
such that the following
holds. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\alpha^{\prime}
-long cover of dimension
\leq N

by
G
-invariant open subsets of
\mathit{FS}
. Then there exists a
partition of unity
\{t_{U}\colon\mathit{FS}\to[0,1]\mid U\in\mathcal{U}\}

subordinate to
\mathcal{U}
and
\delta>0
such that

(a)

for
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathit{FS}
with

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)
we have

|t_{U}(c)-t_{U}(c^{\prime})|<{\varepsilon};

(b)

the
t_{U}
are
G
-invariant.

Proposition
D.7
is
[
6
, Prop. 6.4]
.
Its proof is not complicated. The
fact that the cover is
\alpha^{\prime}
-long allows us to find a partition of
unity whose functions only vary very slowly along flow lines.

Proposition D.8

(Dimension of long thin covers)
.

There is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
\alpha^{\prime}>0
there is

\alpha^{\prime\prime}
such that the following is true. Let
\mathcal{W}
be an

\alpha^{\prime\prime}
-long cover of
\mathit{FS}
by
G
-invariant open subsets. Then
there exist collections
\mathcal{U}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{N}
of open

G
-invariant subsets of
\mathit{FS}
such that

(a)

\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{N}
is an

\alpha^{\prime}
-long cover of
\mathit{FS}
, in particular

\mathcal{U}_{i}\cap\mathcal{U}_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\not=j
;

(b)

for each
i
the open sets in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint;

(c)

for each
U\in\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{N}

there is
W\in\mathcal{W}
with
U\subseteq W
.

Proposition
D.8
is
[
6
, Prop. 6.6]
.
It is closely related to other existence
results for long and thin covers in the literature, although it is not
stated in exactly this form elsewhere. To prove it one can pass to
the quotient
G\backslash\mathit{FS}
and adapt the strategy
from
[
34
]
. The main difference is that
here our constrain on
\mathcal{U}
is formulated in terms of the given
cover
\mathcal{W}
, while in
[
34
]
the
constrain on the members of
\mathcal{U}
are formulated in terms of the
group action.

Proposition D.9

(Local structure)
.

For all
\alpha^{\prime\prime}>0
there are
\beta>0
and

\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.0pt\mathrm{vcy}}
finite with the following property. For
all
\eta>0
and all
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}
there exist
U\subseteq\mathit{FS}

open,
h\colon U\to G
,
V\in\mathcal{V}
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

(a)

for some neighborhood
U_{0}
of the orbit

Gc_{0}
we have
\Phi_{[-\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}]}(U_{0})\subseteq U
;

(b)

U
is
G
-invariant;

(c)

for
c,c^{\prime}\in U
we have

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime})\implies d_{V\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(h(c),h(c^{\prime}))<(\beta,\eta);

(d)

for
c\in U
,
g\in G
we have;

d_{V\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(h(gc),gh(c))<(\beta,\eta).

Proposition
D.9
is
[
6
, Prop. 6.8]
.
We will briefly discuss its proof in
Subsection
D.vi
.

The map
f_{1}
from Theorem
D.3
can locally be
constructed using Proposition
D.9
. Using the partition
of unity from Proposition
D.7
these can be patched
together to a map
\mathit{FS}\to|\mathbf{J}^{N}_{\mathcal{V}}(G)|^{\wedge}
. As noted
already in Subsection
1.m
this patching
procedure forces us to pass from orbits to products of orbits. Some
care is needed to control the dimension of the image of theses maps;
this is where Proposition
D.8
is needed.

D.
vi
.
Local structure of the flow space

For
c\in\mathit{FS}(X)
we set

\displaystyle K_{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle G_{c}=\{g\in G\mid gc=c\}=\{g\in G\mid gc(t)=c(t)\;\text{for all}\;t\in\mathbb{R}\};

\displaystyle V_{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{g\in G\mid\exists t\in\mathbb{R}\,\text{such that}\,gc=\Phi_{t}(c)\};

\displaystyle\tau_{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\inf\{t>0\mid\exists v\in V_{c}\setminus K_{c},\text{with}\;\Phi_{t}(c)=vc\}.

We use
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. If
\tau_{c}<\infty
then we say
that
c
is
periodic
. We have
K_{c}\subseteq V_{c}
as the flow
is
G
-equivariant. For
\alpha>0,\delta>0
,
c\in\mathit{FS}
we set

(D.10)

U^{\mathrm{fol}}_{\alpha,\delta}(c):=\{c^{\prime}\in\mathit{FS}\mid d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)\}.

One may think of
U^{\mathrm{fol}}_{\alpha,\delta}(c)
as an open ball around

c
with respect to
d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
.

Proposition D.11
.

Let
\mathit{FS}_{0}\subseteq\mathit{FS}
be compact. For all

\alpha>0
there is
\beta>0
such that the following is true:
For all
\eta>0
,
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}_{0}
, there are
\delta>0
and a
(not necessarily continuous) map

h\colon G\cdot U^{{\mathrm{fol}}}_{\alpha,\delta}(c_{0})\to G
satisfying

(a)

for

c,c^{\prime}\in G\cdot U^{{\mathrm{fol}}}_{\alpha,\delta}(c_{0})
we have

d_{\mathit{FS}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(c,c^{\prime})<(\alpha,\delta)\quad\implies\quad d_{V_{c_{0}}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(h(c),h(c^{\prime}))<(\beta,\eta);

(b)

for
g\in G
,

c\in G\cdot U^{{\mathrm{fol}}}_{\alpha,\delta}(c_{0})
we have

d_{V_{c_{0}}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}(h(gc),gh(c))<(\beta,\eta).

Sketch of proof.

Let
\alpha>0
be given. Using compactness of
\mathit{FS}_{0}
, it is not
difficult to show the following: there is
\beta>0
such that for

g\in G
,
c\in\mathit{FS}_{0}
we have

d_{\mathit{FS}}(gc,c)<3\alpha\quad\implies\quad d_{G}(g,e)<\beta.

Next let
\eta>0
and
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}_{0}
be given. For
n\in\mathbb{N}

choose
h_{n}\colon G\cdot U_{\alpha,1/n}^{{\mathrm{fol}}}(c_{0})\to G
such
that
c\in h_{n}(c)\cdot U_{\alpha,1/n}^{\mathrm{fol}}(c_{0})
for all

c\in G\cdot U_{\alpha,\delta_{n}}^{{\mathrm{fol}}}(c_{0})
. It is not
difficult to check that for all sufficiently large
n
the map
h_{n}

satisfies
(a)
and
(b)
.
For more details see
[
6
, Prop. 9.2]
.
∎

Remark D.12
.

In the assertion of Proposition
D.11
the estimates
can be strengthened to use
d_{K_{c_{0}}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
in place of

d_{V_{c_{0}}\text{-}\mathrm{fol}}
provided
\tau_{c_{0}}>\ell

where
\ell
is a constant only depending on
\alpha
.

On its own Proposition
D.11
is not quite strong enough
to imply Proposition
D.9
, because it is not quite clear
yet that we only need a finite set
\mathcal{V}
of subgroups. To resolve
this one needs to understand how
V_{c_{0}}
varies in
c_{0}
. Ideally
we would like for
V_{c_{0}}
not to increase in small neighborhoods of

c_{0}
, at least up to conjugation. While we do not know this, we have
the following result.

Proposition D.13
.

There exists
\mathit{FS}_{0}\subseteq\mathit{FS}
compact such that

(a)

G\cdot\mathit{FS}_{0}=\mathit{FS}
;

(b)

for
\ell>0
and
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}_{0}

there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
c_{0}
in
\mathit{FS}_{0}
such
that for all
c\in U
with
\tau_{c}\leq\ell
we have

V_{c}\subseteq V_{c_{0}}
.

Proposition
D.13
is
[
6
, Prop. A.7]
.
We will not discuss its proof in
detail. But we want to point out that this proof uses the
combinatorial structure of the building
X
. Here we fix an apartment

A
for
X
and use that the
G
-action translates any geodesic to a
geodesic in
A
. To study the groups
V_{c_{0}}
for
c_{0}\in\mathit{FS}(A)
,
one can then use the combinatorial structure of
A
as a Coxeter
complex. Here
\mathit{FS}(A)
is the flow space for
A
. This is the only
point where the proof of Theorem
D.1
uses the
combinatorial structure of
X
.

To prove Proposition
D.9
one combines
Proposition
D.11
, Remark
D.12
and
Proposition
D.13
.
This concludes the discussion of Proposition
D.9
.
